- Higher-priced products need more audience reach and more spend to produce one sale, so the daily budget should be sized to the product price. To test fast, set each ad set's daily budget at the BECPP or slightly above it, so some ad sets can produce an early sale as a positive signal. [L0376.004]
- For higher-priced products that need more reach to convert, setting the daily ad set budget at the BECPP or slightly above it speeds up testing, and an early sale is a good sign. [L0375.004]
- Starting with a high daily budget on a new Meta campaign spends money before the pixel and audience have enough data to optimise. Begin with a low daily budget per campaign and raise it only after results show which audiences perform. [L0649.071]
- Daily test spend equals the number of ad sets multiplied by the daily budget per ad set. For example, 5 ad sets at $5 each means $25 per day, and running them for 2 to 4 days gives enough data to decide. [L0774.052]
- Low-budget product testing (procedure): 1. Launch the new product with a low daily budget, for example about $10 per day. 2. Check whether the ads gain traction. 3. If they do not, move to the next product. 4. If they do, test different offers before deciding to scale. [L1185.017]
- To find a winning Facebook ad with a small budget (procedure): 1. Start each new ad with a small daily budget, for example from 5 pounds per day. 2. Increase budget in small steps while watching which creative gets results quickly. 3. Scale only the creative that performs, and stop the rest early. [L0561.059]

### Break-even rules and formulas

#### Break-even cost per purchase (BECPP)

- Break-even cost per purchase is the threshold for an ad set: spend at least that amount before judging it. [L0067.010]
- Let an ad spend up to the break-even cost per purchase, for example the full profit per sale such as 17 USD, before judging it. [L0502.038]
- Spend up to the profit margin per ad before deciding. If an ad is still unprofitable at three times the target cost per acquisition, shut it off. [L0524.036]
- Kill an ad set in testing once it has spent the break-even cost per purchase without a single sale. [L0646.015]
- Testing budget per ad set from the desired CPA: spend three times the desired CPA on each ad before deciding to turn it off or scale it. In the example, a product costing $15 that sells for $45 targets a CPA of about $15, so each ad is tested with $45 spend. Daily ad budgets for testing in the example ranged from $10 to $50 per ad. [L0999.011]
- Testing budget per ad set = 3 x desired CPA. Desired CPA is roughly the amount the business can afford to pay for one customer, based on the margin: with a product cost of $15 and sale price of $45, the margin supports a CPA of about $15. Example: $15 CPA x 3 = $45 spend per ad before judging it. [L0999.012]
- Before deciding to switch off an ad, let it spend up to the target cost per acquisition. For example, if the desired CPA is 15 units and ROAS shows 30 percent margin, spend about three times that before deciding. Then shut the ad off if it does not work. [L0646.035]
- Set the target cost per acquisition at about half the profit margin per order. Example: with a $30 margin, aim for $15 per customer, and cut unprofitable ads once they reach about $45 spent. [L0524.037]
- Check the ad-level spend: if an ad has spent around $8 to $10 or more, or about 1,000 to 1,500 impressions, and shows poor CPC and CTR, it is taking budget from other ads and should be turned off. [L0646.037]

#### Break-even ROAS

- Break-even ROAS = selling price divided by gross profit per sale, where gross profit = selling price minus cost of goods sold (COGS) and fulfilment costs. Example from the lesson: a $30 flower pot with $11 COGS and a price of $29.9 gives a break-even ROAS of 1.6. [L0642.016]
- The break-even ROAS for a given product is set by its margin. A product with a $6 cost sold at about $7 allowed a break-even of about 1.1, while another brand's break-even was 1.5. Calculate break-even ROAS before deciding whether an ad with a given ROAS is profitable. [L0999.058]
- Break-even ROAS and the scaling conversion goal: a conversion goal for a new interest or ad set is at least four to five sales within the next couple of days, with the return on ad spend at the break-even ROAS plus 1. [L0650.008]

### Kill and keep rules

#### When to kill an ad set or interest

- Cut an ad set that has not produced a sale after spending its break-even amount. If the ad set shows promising add-to-carts or initiate checkouts, or a good CTR, let it run one more day before deciding. [L0645.003]
- Use this procedure to decide when to turn off an ad set: 1. Let the ad set spend an amount equal to the cost of goods of the product (for example, $20 for a product costing $20). 2. Analyse the CTR of the ad set. 3. Check whether it brought a sale and whether the return on ad spend (ROAS) is good. 4. If the sale exists and the ROAS is good, keep the ad set running. 5. If not, turn it off and replace it with another interest or audience. [L0562.014]
- After a test in which ABO has lost money, test a new interest in CBO with a $100 budget. Cut it if it has no add to cart after about $15 to $20 spent (for a $40 product with a $25 margin), or if it has no sale after $40 spent. [L0645.045]
- Judge an ad set after roughly $20 to $30 of spend. Pausing it earlier is premature impatience. Once it has passed that spend and still performs poorly, pause it and duplicate the best performing ad set instead. [L0650.002]
- Spend roughly $20 to $30 on an ad set before judging it, because that amount shows whether the cost per acquisition is affordable against the break-even point. Pausing an ad set before it reaches that spend usually means judging it too early. [L0650.055]
- Another operator used a stricter cut-off of about $10 to $15 of spend for a new ad that had not converted, and paused it at that point. This is a faster test than the $20 to $30 guideline and fits a tight starting budget. [L0650.056]
- Wait about four days after publishing, so each ad set spends around $20, then analyse the results. Keep ad sets running only if they meet the thresholds for CTR and cost. [L0642.011]
- Close an ad set in a test if it has spent $10 and its CTR is below 1.5%. If an ad has spent only around $0.30, leave it running because there is too little data. [L0642.012]
- An ad with a CTR around 1% after more than $10 of spend is a weak signal and can be paused. An ad with a clearly higher CTR and low spend (around $4) can be kept running because the spend is still too small for a verdict. [L0650.003]
- Ads with spend of $20 to $30 and no sales can be closed. If all ads produce sales, keep them running together in the same ad set. [L0773.037]
- Remove a creative only when it performs badly in a consistent way across all ad sets. If results are mixed, keep it and retest it before deleting it. [L0692.017]
- Ads that show sales stay in place. [L0773.037]
- Keep ad sets that are profitable, close ad sets that are not, and test a different interest. [L0440.033]
- Profitability cannot be expected immediately. Finding the right audience, the right ad and the right offer on the website usually takes time before an ad set becomes profitable. [L0440.034]
- If CTR stays at or below 0.3 to 0.5 after about $100 in spend, replace the creative. If CTR stays very low after replacing it, stop the product earlier. [L1184.030]
- A winning product shows initial traction within the first week of running Facebook ads. Early positive signals include audience engagement with the creative, website visits, and user actions such as adding to cart or saving to wishlist. These indicators that the audience resonates with the product justify continuing to test that product-audience combination. [L0229.017]
- Dropshipping is about testing rather than overthinking: launch the product, gather data and decide based on the results. If the data shows good CTR and low CPC, the product is worth more investment. If not, move on. [L1172.037]

#### Kill when no add to cart or no sale (combined with rules 1 to 4)

- Kill an ad set if it has made no sale after testing, but apply Rules 1 to 4 first. Details are in the Rules section below. [L0229.005]
- Test procedure step: after spending another BECPP, check whether ROAS is above BE-ROAS. If yes, let it run. If no, kill it. [L0375.011, L0376.012]

#### Kill and reset rules for ads and products

- Judging a new product's ads after a day or two is premature and wastes money on losing ads. Cutting an ad that has spent beyond its profit margin without a sale is the usual decision, but the margin must be checked first. [L0999.013]
- Judging an ad as a failure after $10 to $15 spend with no add-to-carts is premature. First check whether the offer and product page are attractive enough, and test different offers and bundles before turning ads off. [L1220.008]
- Judging a new ad after a few hours on a $5 daily budget is premature, since small budgets need a day or more before the data shows what works. Facebook normally spends well, so give it time before cutting. [L0645.043]
- Do not judge a Facebook ad or ad set before it has spent roughly $20 to $30. [L0650.002]
- Remove an ad set in testing when it spends beyond break-even without a sale, unless it shows add-to-carts or CTR signals. [L0645.003]
- Pushing a product at audiences who do not respond to it leads ad platforms to penalise the advertiser's content. Founders who rely on their own intuition that a product is great, instead of letting the market react, often run into this. [L0760.027]
- Running ads in a market with strong competition is normal: some advertisers scale a product in a market while others run only a single ad for it. A single ad in a market does not prove the product fails there. [L0691.012]
- Cutting ad creatives and age groups too quickly after a few good sales can cause CTR and sales to drop sharply, so changes to a working test need careful evaluation rather than sudden large cuts. [L0690.018]
- Test a product for at least the spend set out in its budget before deciding, and do not replace a product after one bad day. [L1124.007]

### Rules 1 to 4 and safety rule

Source C decision rules for an ad set after launch. Apply them in order.

1. **Rule 1:** Allow each ad set to spend its break-even cost per purchase before making optimisation decisions, OR let it run for three days, whichever comes later. For products with margins under $20, prioritise the three-day minimum to accumulate sufficient data. [L0229.001]
2. **Rule 2:** After Rule 1 is complete, check whether the ad set generated at least one sale.
   - If yes, let it continue spending another full break-even cost per purchase amount.
   - If no sale occurred, check the CPC. If CPC is below $1.50, extend the ad set for one additional day. If CPC is $1.50 or above, deactivate the ad set and replace it with a new interest. [L0229.005]
3. **Replacement procedure:** To replace an underperforming ad set while testing new interests:
   1. Identify the ad set that achieved at least one sale (the winning ad set).
   2. Duplicate that winning ad set.
   3. Open the Audience section and click the "Suggested" button to see interest recommendations based on the current audience.
   4. Select a new suggested interest (e.g., if current is "Carpet," try "Living Room" or related suggestions).
   5. Verify the audience size is reasonable (e.g., 100+ million for broad targeting).
   6. Click Publish to launch the new ad set.
   7. Deactivate the old underperforming ad set.
   8. Apply all rules starting from Rule 1 to this newly created ad set. [L0229.006]
4. **Rule 3:** If an ad set survived Rule 2 by receiving a one-day extension but made no sale within that 24-hour period, deactivate it and replace it using the same duplication and interest-suggestion method described in Rule 2, starting the new ad set through Rule 1 again. [L0229.007]
5. **Rule 4:** Once an ad set clears Rules 2 and 3, allow it to spend an additional amount equal to double the break-even cost per purchase (not double the total, but double the initial threshold). By the time this additional amount is spent, the ad set must achieve a return on ad spend (ROAS) equal to or higher than its break-even ROAS. If it falls below break-even ROAS at that point, deactivate and replace it. [L0229.008]

**Safety rule:** If after running ads for three to four days your entire campaign shows zero sales across all ad sets, stop the campaign immediately. Do not continue spending in hopes of organic improvement. Proceed to troubleshooting instead. [L0229.010]

**Safety rule for a campaign with no sales:** Pause a campaign immediately if no sale occurs within the first $50 of spend. [L0298.006]

**Reduce, do not kill outright:** Reduce budget if results are consistently poor for the last 4 days. Test a reduced version before completely pausing. [L0298.015]

**First-day rule for high-budget campaigns:** Pause the campaign for the day if it spends twice the break-even cost per purchase without producing a sale. For example, with a break-even CPA of $30 and a $100 launch budget, turn it off once it has spent $60 without a purchase. [L1335.032]

**Sources disagree on the first-day stop.** The Source D 2026 course says not to cut a product after a single day or after only $50 without sales, because Meta needs time to test segments. [L0037.007] The Source C safety rule stops a campaign with no sale within $50 of spend. [L0298.006] The first applies to deciding whether to cut a product. The second applies to a campaign running with no sales. For a high-budget campaign, the 2x break-even stop at [L1335.032] applies. The Source D case in which day one looked like failure and became profitable on day three shows why a day-one cut can be wrong. [L1124.017]

**Patience against day-one panic.** A new Meta test with a high cost per purchase on day one should not be switched off right away, because one day of spend and a single sale is too little data to judge a product. [L1124.001] Cutting a test after the first day with a high CPA and moving on to the next product, a habit known as paper handing, often kills products that would have become profitable. Judging a product on the first day alone is the main reason good tests get lost. [L1124.002]

### Scaling thresholds

- Scale an ad set once it has produced around four purchases. [L0524.035]
- Scale an interest only after it reaches the sales threshold set in advance (for example six to seven sales) and is profitable. [L0361.022]
- If any running ad set brings 4 or more profitable sales, scale it. [L0375.012, L0376.012]
- Once a sale comes in, spend up to the break-even cost per purchase again. [L0502.038]
- Example of a scaling outcome: a new product runs five ad sets at about $12 each. Three are switched off for weak performance, and the other two keep running until they reach seven profitable sales, which can take about one to one and a half weeks. [L1107.029]
- When scaling into CBO, look for at least three purchases on the ad set before trusting the result. [L0645.008]
- Test a new product with interest targeting first. Once it reaches roughly 10 to 20 to 30 sales, add an Advantage+ broad campaign alongside it. [L1161.023]
- Once a sale starts, the ads work: find the winning interest, country and offer. [L1161.020]
- Keep ad sets with sales running, and put the budget behind the country that performs best rather than spreading it evenly. [L1220.004]
- Scale only the creative that performs, and stop the rest early. [L0561.059]

### Decision benchmarks: CTR, CPC and CPM

| Metric or threshold | Value in source | Applies to | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Industry CPC range | $1.00 to $1.50 | Benchmark for whether an underperforming ad set shows promise or should be replaced | L0229.004 |
| Rule 2 CPC cut | Below $1.50 = extend one day; $1.50 or above = replace | Source C Rules 1 to 4 path | L0229.005 |
| Test benchmark | CTR above 2% and CPC below $1 | Benchmark; if not reached, improve creative or offer before scaling spend | L1219.014 |
| Interest test decision | CPC below $1 and CTR above 2% = one more day | Interest-test decision procedure (see below) | L0375.011, L0376.012 |
| Close ad set | Spent $10 and CTR below 1.5% | Ad set in a test | L0642.012 |
| Weak ad signal | CTR around 1% after more than $10 | Can be paused | L0650.003 |
| Creative replacement | CTR at or below 0.3 to 0.5 after about $100 | Replace creative | L1184.030 |
| Judge ad | Spend about $8 to $10 and 1,000 to 3,000 impressions | Individual ad; $1 to $3 is not reliable | L1345.076 |
| Poor ad level | About $8 to $10 spend or 1,000 to 1,500 impressions, poor CPC and CTR | Turn off | L0646.037 |

- **Sources disagree on the CPC and CTR thresholds.** The Source C Rule 2 uses a CPC of $1.50 as the replace line. [L0229.005] The interest-test decision uses CPC below $1 and CTR above 2% as the keep-one-more-day line. [L0375.011, L0376.012] The $1.50 line applies within Rules 1 to 4. The $1 and 2% line applies to the interest-test decision after BECPP. Both are given as stated.
- **Check CPC, CTR and CPM together.** If the CPC is too high, the CTR too low and the CPM too high, and there are no sales, remove that ad set and replace it. [L0502.038]
- **Read metrics per country.** Compare CTR, CPC, CPM and conversion rate per country. Narrow the test to the countries with the best results. [L1185.022]
- **Judge on CTR and CPC first.** Judge the ads by CTR and CPC first. If the ad metrics are good but there are no sales, change the store. If there are no add-to-carts, first test the offer and bundles before concluding the ads fail. [L1220.007]
- **CTR as an interest signal.** A good CTR means the product audience is interested and the product can continue. A bad CTR means the targeted audience does not care, so switch to another product. [L1184.037]
- **Check the store data after the first $100.** If ad metrics are good but there are no sales or add-to-carts, the problem is the store. [L1161.020]
- Add-to-carts are a useful early indicator that an ad is performing. [L0999.027]
- Use the Source D 2026 check of CPM, CTR and CPC after the first $100, then read add-to-carts and sales. [L1161.020]
- Facebook ad results are inconsistent, including at large budgets. Expect good and bad days, and judge performance over longer periods rather than reacting to single-day results. [L0690.015]
- The break-even ROAS and ROAS checks for scaling are in the break-even section. [L0645.008, L0650.008, L0646.035]

### Timing and test duration

#### Minimum duration by source

| Duration | Context | Source |
|---|---|---|
| 3 to 4 days minimum before cutting | Source D 2026 minimum; exception: if day 1 and day 2 are both poor and coaches advise cutting, that overrides the minimum | L0037.006 |
| 3 days minimum | Source C procedure, at break-even cost per purchase spend | L0067.012 |
| 2 to 3 days | Let a newly tested audience run instead of judging after one day | L0692.001 |
| 2 to 3 days (usually) | Each ad set spends at least BECPP | L0375.011, L0376.012 |
| 3 to 4 days | Look at 3 to 4 days of ROAS data before deciding | L0645.008 |
| About 4 days | Wait until each ad set has spent around $20 | L0642.011, L1220.005 |
| 4 days or more | Each ad set runs at least 4 days | L1184.028 |
| 2 to 4 days | Run five ad sets before analysing data | L0774.034 |
| 7 days | Evergreen Facebook campaign, before judging or cutting | L1016.056, L1016.054 |
| 48 to 72 hours | Phase 5 initial test, evaluation window | L0199.011 |

- Do not decide to scale or kill an ad set after only three days, because that is too early. [L0693.031]
- Let each ad set spend at least BECPP, usually over 2 to 3 days. [L0375.011, L0376.012]
- Let an evergreen Facebook campaign run for about 7 days before judging it or cutting the budget, because shorter windows do not show reliable results. [L1016.056]
- Let an evergreen campaign run for about 7 days before judging it. Shorter windows do not show reliable results. [L1016.054]
- Leave the test running for several days and read the trend before deciding. [L1124.001]
- Let each ad set spend the amount set out above before judging it; when the amount cannot be reached quickly, extend the test. [L0642.011]
- Phase 5 (Days 60-75) of the Source D course prioritises analytics data over profit. The goal is to determine whether a product is worth running, to identify whether issues stem from ad or funnel, and to build operational knowledge for scaling. [L0199.010]

#### Day-of-week and schedule

- Product-testing campaigns are commonly run from Thursday to Sunday. Running them on Monday to Wednesday is acceptable too, as long as the data is broken down by country, age group and gender (if several genders are tested) afterwards. [L1220.003]
- Do not assume that weekends or Thursday to Sunday are always the best days to test audiences. Day-of-week patterns differed strongly between months and products, so check your own data over a longer period before using them. [L0692.002]
- Spread a test budget across every day of the week rather than a short burst. A three-day run misses buyers who are paid on a particular day, such as Thursday, and gives weaker data. [L1225.093]
- Schedule the start for the next day at 12:00 AM in the ad account time zone. [L0648.001]

#### Editing during the test window

- Once a test campaign is running and the store, creatives and product page are ready, do not edit the campaign during the test window. In the example, leaving every setting untouched for two days brought CPA down sharply on its own. [L1124.004]
- Source D day checks (procedure): 1. Launch a CBO test with three ad sets at a $100 daily budget, with all creatives placed in each ad set. 2. Make no edits to the campaign for three days. 3. Compare each day's CPA against the break-even CPA for the product. 4. Once the campaign produces purchases below break-even, scale it (the scaling method was not covered in the lesson). [L1124.006]
- CBO test rhythm: let the test run for two days, check the metrics and P&L on days 1 and 2, and on day 3 scale only if the product is green and the margins are good. Keep the same CBO and duplicate it with new creatives on day 3 instead of restarting. [L0348.011]
- Changing audience, placement or other ad set settings during a product test muddles the results. Keep default settings unless a legal or practical constraint requires a change. [L1193.016]
- Keep ad sets under review by checking daily data on creative, audience and trend when a CBO runs at $100 or more per day. [L1345.074]

#### Product rhythm

- Run product tests in a repeating loop: test a product, give it enough time and budget to read the result, and only then move on to the next product. Do not replace a product after one bad day. [L1124.007]
- Testing a product: products are not given a fixed budget in advance. If a product is not profitable, switch to the next product. [L0440.033]
- If no ad set brings any sale after testing at least 10 interests, switch to the next product. [L0375.011, L0376.012]

### Store versus ad diagnosis

- The job of the ads is to drive traffic, and the job of the store is to make the sale. If traffic arrives but there are no sales, change the store (design, offer, bundle, pricing or copy) before changing the ads. [L1220.006]
- Before launching a test, make sure the store has a branded product page and updated, good-looking creatives. A weak store or old creatives make the test result less reliable. [L1124.008]
- Testing procedure with checkpoints (procedure): 1. Set a test budget of $300 per product and split it into blocks of $100. 2. Spend the first $100, then check CPM, CTR and CPC in Ads Manager. 3. Check the store data after the first $100: if ad metrics are good but there are no sales or add-to-carts, the problem is the store. Change the theme, price, offer or copywriting and spend another $100. 4. If the ads have no good metrics, change the audience or the creatives and test again with the next $100. 5. Once sales start, the ads work: find the winning interest, country and offer. 6. If the store is changed and there are still no sales after the tweaks, and the offer seems good, move on to the next product. [L1161.020]
- Spending the full $300 on a product in one go, without a checkpoint, makes it impossible to tell whether the ads or the store are failing. Split the budget into $100 blocks and check the data after each block. [L1161.021]
- Ad account and payment check (procedure): 1. Check the ad account and payment setup after buying an existing Facebook account, especially if it had a restriction. 2. If spend stays far below the budget, duplicate the campaign and run it again. 3. Let each ad set run about 4 days until it has about $20 spent. 4. Judge the ads by CTR and CPC first. 5. If the ad metrics are good but there are no sales, change the store. 6. If there are no add-to-carts, first test the offer and bundles before concluding the ads fail. [L1220.007]
- Debugging weak results in new international markets (procedure): 1. Check the market settings and shipping settings for each market. 2. Place a test order through the checkout using two random addresses from each country to confirm it works end to end. 3. Break down the ad results by country in the ads dashboard (age and gender are also possible breakdowns). 4. Find the country that received most of the spend but produced no sales, and pause it. 5. Test the remaining countries, ideally one at a time, and check the results again. 6. Check the store for broken buttons and wrong language text. 7. Review the full funnel, including add to cart and checkout steps, to find where visitors drop off. [L0694.004]
- A market that looks dead after a small spend may just lack data, so split tests by country in a way that gives each one enough spend. [L0694.003]
- Troubleshoot a campaign with zero clicks before building broader audiences. Do not start top-of-funnel Facebook ads while a campaign is getting zero clicks. Troubleshoot that campaign first, or turn it off and restart. (Source A, 2020, older advice.) [L0852.057]
- Ad campaign with no clicks: a case in which a campaign targeting Australia spent 143 USD in total. [L0361.051]
- Dropshipping rests on the funnel: a case in which ads showed CTR of 3.1 to 5.45 percent, but zero add-to-carts and zero Shopify analytics. [L0361.051]
- Check the store and the offer after strong CTR without sales, using the funnel review step. [L0361.051]

#### Case: cheap clicks, no add to cart (complete)

Case: a student ran a Meta campaign targeting Australia only and spent 143 USD in total. CTR ranged from 3.1 to 5.45 percent across the assets, and CPC ranged from 0.60 to 1.15 USD with an average of 0.67 USD. The campaign produced zero add-to-carts, and Shopify analytics also showed zero, so the pixel alone was not the likely cause. The advice was to stop the campaign, review the funnel with the mentor, and look for a new product, because cheap clicks without add-to-carts show weak interest in the product or offer. [L0361.051]

#### Case: dominant creative and suspected tracking fault (complete)

Case: a student ran a Meta campaign for three days, spending 150 EUR across five ad sets until the spend reached break-even. Most of the budget went to one video creative, and Meta kept sending the spend to it. The store's general conversion rate was about 1 to 1.1 percent. The campaign produced 91 clicks at a CPC of 1.76 EUR and a CTR of 1.08 percent, but only one add-to-cart. The student stopped the ads and cut the dominant creative from the ad sets. The mentor suspected a tracking fault, a funnel problem, or an outdated product, and noted that influencer traffic had produced more add-to-carts and at least one purchase. [L0361.050]

#### Case: day one failure that became profitable (complete)

Starting point: a new dropshipping product was tested with a campaign set up as CBO, with three ad sets, all creatives placed in each ad set, a $100 daily budget, and a branded product page with updated creatives. Day 1: the campaign spent about $104.49 and produced one purchase, giving a CPA of $104.49, which would normally lead to switching it off. Day 2: nothing was edited and CPA fell to about a quarter of day one (around $26). Day 3: nothing was edited again, and the campaign produced about $1,000 in revenue at about $15 CPA, with more purchases than Facebook reported. Day 4 morning: CPA started at about $4. Break-even CPA was around $20 to $21 with a $30 selling price and a $10 product cost. Result: the test looked like a failure on day one but became profitable by leaving it running, and the winning campaign was then scaled. The speaker estimated roughly $200 profit on day three. These figures are as stated in the lesson. [L1124.017]

### Testing variables and order

- When testing, fix the variables in this order: 1) the offer and the website at the end of the funnel, 2) the audience, and only then 3) vary the creative. Otherwise a weak result cannot be traced to a single cause. [L0646.061]
- Facebook test structure (procedure): see the standard ABO product test above; all settings identical except the interest. [L0648.002]
- Isolation rule for ads: when Facebook favours one ad over others in an ad set, initially trust Facebook's choice. If you want to test underperforming ads, isolate them in a separate ad set and force spend into them for 3 days to see if they perform better in isolation. [L0067.024]
- Ad testing procedure in an ad set (procedure): 1. Create 3 ads in one interest ad set. 2. Run for 3 days minimum at break-even cost per purchase spend level. 3. Review which ad Facebook favours by looking at spend and CTR. 4. If one ad significantly underperforms but other metrics are promising, isolate it in a separate ad set and force spend into it to test further. 5. If metrics across the ad set look good overall (good attention and interest), proceed to creative review and hypothesis testing. 6. Do 2 to 3 rounds of testing iterations minimum before killing a product. [L0067.012]
- Ad testing with an isolated ad: a per-ad test with high spend in one set needs a separate ad set to read the ad itself. [L0067.024]
- Split test interests, mobile, desktop, operating system, gender and age brackets as much as you can. [L0643.050]
- Start new campaigns with a low budget and split test one variable at a time, choosing creative as the variable when creating the test. [L0649.027]
- Test at least 3 to 5 ad sets per product before judging. [L0375.002]
- Keep the same creatives across ad sets so the interest is the only variable. [L0376.034]
- Do not test many variables at once. Change one thing per ad, keep copy and hook identical, and compare the lift. [L0640.031]
- Source D 2026 product rule: test one pixel per product; if a product in the test fails several times in a row, move the successful ad set to a CBO and turn off the failing ad set. [L0348.020]

### Country and market tests

- Multi-country market testing (procedure): 1) Launch the product in about five countries, including the countries where the competitor runs ads. 2) Break down ad results by country. 3) Compare CTR, CPC, CPM and conversion rate per country. 4) Narrow the test to the countries with the best results. [L1185.022]
- For each country, spend at least $50 in total, with at least $10 on each of five ad sets, before judging a product. This gives enough data to read CTR. [L0661.035]
- Put the budget behind the country that performs best, rather than spreading it evenly. [L1220.004]
- Split test by country, age group and gender after a test campaign has run. [L1220.004]

### Testing pitfalls

- Do not cut a product after a single day or after spending only $50 without sales. Meta requires time to test different audience segments and show results. [L0037.007]
- Judging a new ad after a few hours on a $5 daily budget is premature. [L0645.043]
- Cutting ad creatives and age groups too quickly after a few good sales can cause CTR and sales to drop sharply. [L0690.018]
- Assuming weekends are always the best days to test. [L0692.002]
- Removing a creative after mixed results, rather than retesting it. [L0692.017]
- Running a product for a full month across carousel, image and video creatives with no sale, rather than setting a fixed cap. [L0660.046]
- Spreading a small budget across many ad sets. [L0694.003]
- Pushing a product at audiences who do not respond. [L0760.027]
- Spending the full $300 on a product in one go, without a checkpoint. [L1161.021]
- Changing audience, placement or ad set settings during a product test. [L1193.016]
- Assuming a method working in one ad account works in another. [L0375.031]
- Judging an ad as a failure after $10 to $15 spend with no add-to-carts. [L1220.008]
- Cutting a test after the first day with a high CPA (paper handing). [L1124.002]
- Judging a product on the first day alone. [L1124.002]
- Judging a product after one bad day, and not giving a product the spend to read its result. [L1124.007]
- Judging an interest, ad or ad set before its break-even spend. [L0645.003, L0646.015]
- Reacting to single-day results. [L0690.015]
- Running ads with no measurable goal, for example mixing multiple platforms into one store. [L1184.031]
- Under-funding a test with $20 to $40 per campaign. [L1124.005]
- Scaling before the threshold of sales is reached. [L0361.022]
- A mixed approach where results are judged before the ad has spent enough to read a result, while also not stopping when the spend passes break-even with no promising signal. [L0640.051]
- Judging a new product's ads after a day or two, which wastes money on losing ads. [L0999.013]
- Using a product's ads as proof that the product fails in a market when only one ad was run there. [L0691.012]

### Product-level testing principles

- Dropshipping is about testing rather than overthinking. Launch the product, gather data and decide based on the results. [L1172.037]
- Profitability cannot be expected immediately. [L0440.034]
- Test a product for enough spend before moving on, and keep a product whose metrics show that its audience responds. [L0229.017, L1124.007]
- Products are not given a fixed budget in advance. [L0440.033]
- Results from a test can be read by country, age and gender once spend is split. [L1220.004]

### Before you spend: store and supplier checks
- The landing page must deliver what the Facebook ad promises. If a visitor clicks an ad for one product and the homepage shows unrelated products, the visitor loses trust and leaves. [L1346.030]
- Cut ad spend on products from a supplier who does not process orders at once. Spending to acquire customers for products you cannot ship only wastes budget. [L1407.012]

### Diagnosis order: ad metrics before store conversion
- When checking Meta ads, judge CTR, CPC, and CPM first, and only then the store conversion. [L1345.082]
- Record results daily, including trends and patterns by day of week and time of day, even when sales are flat. [L1345.082]

### Killing underperforming creatives (ad level)
1. Compare each ad's CPC and CTR with your account averages (example: average CPC $0.90, average CTR 1.3%). [L1345.077]
2. Wait until the ad has spent about $8 or more. [L1345.077]
3. If the ad shows clearly worse data, such as a CPC of about $3 and a CTR of 0.3% to 0.5%, turn it off. [L1345.077]
4. Keep the other creatives in the ad set running so they get budget. [L1345.077]

### Killing underperforming ad sets
- Cut an ad set after two days if it has spent more than the breakeven cost per purchase without a sale, and replace it with a new audience. [L1345.078]

### Testing pitfalls
- A typical beginner mistake is launching ads to an unreviewed store and spending money, such as $200 with no sales, before knowing whether the product page, offer or checkout is the cause. [L1452.007]

#### Further details

- The testing phase is built to find winners quickly while not wasting much money or time forcing an ad set to work. Clear kill rules, applied on schedule, are the main protection against overspending on weak audiences or creatives. [L0376.035]

## Ad Creative, Copy and Placement Formats

### Ad Text Anatomy and Field Priorities

- A Facebook ad has three text elements: Primary Text above the creative, Headline in bold below the creative, and Description in small text below the Headline. [L0204.001]
- The Description is often hidden on Reels and Stories placements. [L0204.001]
- Primary Text and Headline are the two most important elements because they stay visible in all placements. Description visibility is unreliable, so optimise the first two first. [L0204.002]
- Viewers see only the first 1-2 lines of Primary Text before deciding to watch the video or skip it. These lines must stop the scroll with a bold claim, a direct question about a pain point, or a benefit statement. [L0204.003]
- Put the website URL in the Primary Text field so users can click from the copy straight to the store. [L0205.007]
- Users see the video first and the text overlay second. Ad copy must not look pushy, so the whole ad reads as organic and native to the platform. [L0205.008]
- (Older, 2021-22.) In a Facebook video ad, the headline and some call-to-action buttons below the image disappear, so the website link belongs at the top of the Primary Text. [L0488.010]

### Primary Text Length and Templates

- Long-form Primary Text runs more than 5-6 lines. Short-form runs 3-4 lines. [L0204.005]
- Select copy length by audience age: for 40+, use long-form copy; for under 40, test short-format copy. [L0204.006]
- Conflicting age thresholds exist in the sources. L0204.006 gives 40+ for long-form and under 40 for short-format. L0637.055 gives above 40 or 50 for Facebook-style captions with product, solution and benefit messaging, and under 40 or 30 for TikTok-style UGC. Use the threshold that matches your audience data. [L0204.006, L0637.055]

Long-form Primary Text template [L0204.007]:

```
Line 1 (bold claim or problem): Introducing [Product Name], the ultimate solution for [Customer Problem].

Line 2-3 (agitation): Are you tired of [specific pain point]? We have got the perfect solution for you. Say goodbye to [problem] once and for all with our innovative [product category].

Line 4 (benefits): Key benefits: [Benefit 1] • [Benefit 2] • [Benefit 3]

Line 5 (offer): [Discount]% off, limited time only / [Discount]% off with code [CODE]

Line 6 (action + outcome): Shop now and transform your [problem] into [desired outcome].
```

Short-form Primary Text template [L0204.008]:

```
Line 1 (problem question): Struggling with [pain point]?

Line 2 (solution intro): Say hello to [Product Name], your answer to [problem].

Line 3 (benefits): [Benefit 1] • [Benefit 2] • [Benefit 3]

Line 4 (exclusive offer): [Discount]% off, exclusive offer

Line 5 (CTA): Shop now
```

### Headlines and Descriptions

Headline formula library [L0204.013]:

```
**Attention-grabbing:** Discover [Product Name] [your solution to problem] OR Get ready to transform your life with [Product Name].

**Urgency + offer:** Limited time offer: [Discount]% off OR Hurry, [Product Name] sales ending soon. Grab [Product Name] now!

**Benefit-driven:** Experience [key benefit] with [Product Name] OR [Key benefit] way to [solve problem]

**Problem-solving:** Say goodbye to [problem] with [Product Name] OR Tired of [customer pain point]? Try [Product Name].
```

- The Description is a small, often-hidden field. Fill it with limited-time offer details (for example "Free shipping" or "30-day money-back guarantee") or social proof (for example "Rated 4.8 out of 5 by 15,000+ happy customers"). Avoid long text. [L0204.014]
- (Older, 2021-22.) On the Facebook news feed, the ad copy can be more detailed and name both the problem and the solution the content offers. The Headline and CTA stay the same as in other placements. [L0415.006]

### Writing and Sourcing Copy

- Gather copy inspiration from three sources: customer psychology research (deepest desires and insecurities), direct customer interviews and feedback, and competitor ad analysis via the Facebook Ads Library. [L0204.009]
- ChatGPT and Copy.ai are free tools for drafting Primary Text and Headlines. Enter your product details and customer pain points, or paste a competitor ad and ask for a rephrased version for your product. Generate 3-5 variations. [L0204.012]
- Lead with the customer's transformation, not the mechanism. "Includes 360-degree compression technology and cooling materials" is weaker than "Migraine relief you can feel in minutes" because the customer cares about the result. [L0313.011]
- Symptom-led headlines name the visible problem the buyer already feels, such as leaking, damage or lost time, rather than the root cause, because people recognise symptoms more easily than causes. [L0701.034]
  - Formula: [symptom the buyer sees] + [what it costs them in time, money or image] + question. [L0701.034]
  - Examples: "Shoes still soaking after your morning walk?" and "Keep finding ruined shirts after the rain? Stop losing money on replacements." [L0701.034]
- Avoid direct claims such as weight-loss guarantees or fitness guarantees in captions, voiceover and on-screen text. Rephrase them so they do not read as a violation. [L0871.073]

### Hooks

- Meta defines the hook as the first one to three seconds of an ad. Other platforms and sources use different time definitions, so the exact length matters less than the element that grabs attention immediately. [L0508.011]
- Hook length in the sources:
  - First 1-3 seconds: Meta's definition. [L0508.011]
  - First 1-5 seconds: the proven hook to reuse from a competitor's ad. [L0637.015]
  - First 3-5 seconds: the part that differs between creative variations in testing. [L0648.060]
- Test idea, style and hook before smaller variables. These three have the largest impact on results even though they take more effort to change. After Meta's Andromeda update, this lets the platform test a wide range of very different ads. [L0508.015]
- Use the Hook Test strategy on Meta, Facebook and Instagram: run several different ad hooks or angles and keep the ones that scale. [L0914.010]
- Judge hooks by hook rate to find the best-performing hook. [L0902.022]

### Offers, Urgency and Guarantees

- Risk-reversal structure (works because it removes fear of wasted money and sets explicit expectations for when to judge results) [L0313.005]:
  1. State the specific benefit timeline ("relief in as little as 7 days").
  2. Include a money-back guarantee with an exact period ("30-night risk-free trial").
  3. Show the price clearly and prominently.
  - Examples: "Feel the difference in 5 days. 60-night money-back guarantee, $44.99" and "Sleep better by night one. 90-night satisfaction guarantee, $59.99 today." [L0313.005]
- Competitor comparison with negative indicators (works by making the alternative look worse and clarifying your unique safety or benefit advantage) [L0313.007]:
  1. Display your product's key attributes with checkmarks or positive phrasing.
  2. List what alternatives include that customers want to avoid, marked with X, "no" or "risk of".
  - Examples: "Natural formula ✓, Zero chemicals X, Long-lasting ✓, No rebound effect X" and "Effective immediately ✓, No grogginess X, No prescription X, No dependency ✓." [L0313.007]
- Urgency with specificity (vague urgency such as "soon" creates no action, while specific deadlines trigger fear of missing out) [L0313.008]:
  - State the end day, for example "Offer ends Sunday".
  - Or use "Today Only" or "Limited time only".
  - Or show inventory scarcity.
  - Examples: "Launch offer ends Thursday. Get 20% off now" and "This weekend only: buy now at launch price, $39.99." [L0313.008]
- Offer testing: start without an offer, check visitor behaviour, then test offers such as Buy one get the second item 20% off, Buy one get the second 50% off, or Buy two get the third free. The presenter's winning offer at the time was Buy one get the second item 20% off. [L0642.027]
- Place the offer text in the Primary Text of a single image ad, the most visible spot, where it also appears as the first line on Instagram. For traffic from both Facebook and Instagram, put the offer in the Primary Text. It can also go in other parts of the ad. [L0650.018]
- Run the first ads as conversion campaigns with urgency and fear-of-missing-out messaging so first-time visitors buy and the product is validated. Use retargeting and email to win back visitors who did not buy. [L0661.036]
- Use only evergreen promo codes, such as a permanent 3% or 5% code created in Shopify. Expiring or changing codes break remarketing ads once they stop working. Confirm codes are active before publishing. [L1029.025]

### Social Proof, Reviews and Comments

- (Older, 2021-22.) Include text-based customer reviews as quotes under the initial copy in Facebook ads and other ads with enough copy space. This adds credibility and increases curiosity and click-through motivation. [L0241.007]
- (Older, 2021-22.) Review-led ad structure [L1398.005]:
  1. Place a headline that summarises a review above the body copy.
  2. Start the body copy with a review text, so the shopper sees social proof first.
- (Older, 2021-22.) A Facebook ad can carry four or five reviews in the top text, ending with a link back to the product. Choose reviews that highlight different aspects of the product or service so all key points are covered. [L1398.006]
- (Older, 2021-22.) If many reviews praise the business itself, such as seller service or the buying experience, the trusted advisor ad can focus on the business instead of one product. Product images can still be used, but the text should say plainly what the business is known for. [L1398.007]
- Social proof scale: "50K+ users" stated as "relieved and relaxed" gives concrete credibility, compared with an unspecified "thousands of happy customers." [L0313.021]
- Reply to every comment with helpful answers such as sizes or shipping times. Do not pitch or post sales links. Ask a follow-up question to keep the conversation going, which builds engagement that lowers CPM. [L0645.027]
- CPM was reported to fall from about $15 at launch to $5-$8 after building engagement with comments. Typical dropshipping CPMs are $20-$30. A new campaign can still show $20-$30 before it gets engagement. [L0645.028]
- An ad with zero comments looks untrustworthy, so people do not ask questions or comment. Seed the first comments and reviews yourself from the brand page at launch. [L0645.030]
- Ads with high engagement, such as comments, likes and shares, get a lower CPM and reach more people for the same spend. Creatives that invite viewers to comment or share can produce this effect. [L1171.013]
- Example: an ad inviting viewers to post a photo of their own pet in the comments triggered many comments and likes. The higher engagement lowered the CPM, so the same budget reached more people. [L1171.054]

### Single Image Ads

- Image ads are cheaper on Facebook and often convert better than video. Use images for products such as jewellery, fashion and some lifestyle products. [L0577.039]
- Image ads often have a lower CPM than video, so they reach more people for the same budget. Facebook may also show image ads more to users with a slow internet connection. Video ads cost more but can be useful for retargeting. [L0642.019]
- Products that need no explanation, such as home decor, clothing, shoes and jewellery, can be sold with picture ads without video. Videos are needed for products that must be explained or demonstrated. [L0642.020]
- Scaling with image ads alone is possible. One operator reached seven-figure revenue over six-plus months, with roughly 99% of sales from static image ads and no video ads. Image ads are easy to produce in Canva and carry better targeting than many alternatives. [L0349.001]
- For Facebook ads promoting clothing, image ads perform best compared with other formats. [L0779.014]
- Include natural, non-graphic photo ads alongside designed graphics, because designed ads can still look like ads. Carousel ads suit lifestyle-heavy products such as fashion, and single image ads can also be strong. [L0341.020]
- For a single image ad, Meta's Ad Setup > Format offers Single image or video. Use Carousel only when several cards are needed on purpose. [L0865.039]
- Meme ad procedure: place a text on top of the image, add the product picture below, and add an emoji. The presenter builds these in Canva. Test multiple texts with one image. [L0642.023]

### Video Ads

- (Older, 2021-22.) A Facebook video ad is built in almost the same way as a static image ad. A static image version also works, though video is generally preferred by audiences. [L0488.012]
- Video length in the owner's clothing test: ads from 15 seconds to under 20 seconds, with the benefit delivered immediately, tended to outperform one-minute videos, with better click-through and conversion. A before-and-after format showing the result early, then the product, drove more clicks. A one-minute video still has value for retargeting viewers who watched more than 50% of it. [L1077.014]
- Repurposing a standard Facebook ad into a TikTok aspect ratio performed very badly. Even uploaded as an organic video it got fewer than 100 views. Build creative native to each platform instead. [L1077.013]
- Ad owner's early lesson: the early Facebook ads worked because the advertiser was also a target customer who knew the audience's interests and followed the same creators. Use a hook-pain-solution-benefit structure and show the product's benefits directly. [L1077.012]
- Paid ads follow the same content logic as organic posts, because the ad only adds paid distribution and audience targeting. Content that performs organically tends to perform as ads with the right targeting. [L1217.046]
- Transcript procedure for video ads (older, 2021-22):
  1. Get the video transcribed with an automatic online transcription tool such as Rev.
  2. Paste the exact transcript into the ad copy at the top.
  - Reason: some people do not listen to audio, so they can read the message instead. Always include a link to the page you want visitors to reach. [L0488.011]
- Rev is an online transcription tool that automatically turns video audio into text, useful for producing ad copy from video. [L0488.013]
- (Older, 2021-22.) Facebook ads do not offer a click-to-call button. A call-to-action that asks people to phone the business must link to the website's contact page instead. Use the contact page URL as the ad destination. [L0488.015]
- To promote an existing post, use the version that already has the engagement. Likes, comments and views carry over from the original post, but the other platform's copy starts at zero. Example: use the Facebook post if it has far more views than the Instagram copy. [L0865.038]

### Carousel, Catalog and Collection Ads

- Carousel ads perform best on Facebook, and carousels with five cards perform very well. Catalog ads also work well on Facebook. [L0577.038]
- Carousel setup:
  - Pick 3 to 5 product images. The presenter uses 4. [L0642.021]
  - Add an offer and a link to each card. Free shipping text is optional. [L0642.021]
  - Use the collection page link as the destination. [L0642.021]
  - Add a headline and description to each card. [L0642.021]
  - Use the same Primary Text as the single image ad. [L0642.021]
  - CTA: Shop Now or See more. [L0642.021]
- Carousel ads perform well for product tests and show several product angles in one ad. [L1184.032]
- (Older, 2021-22.) Use a single image or video for most Facebook retargeting ads. Collection ads tend to underperform, and a carousel is an option only when several product views need to be shown. [L1342.030]
- Collection ads combine a video or slideshow at the top with dynamic product ads underneath. Shoppers can click through to the exact product they viewed, which makes them suitable for retargeting. [L1243.022]

### Slideshow Ads

- Slideshow ad procedure in Meta Ads Manager [L0642.022]:
  1. Choose Create video instead of image or video.
  2. Select 4 product images.
  3. Click Edit and set the display time per image to 1 second.
  4. Arrange the image order.
  5. Click Play to preview.
  6. Click Save to computer and wait for the video to render.
  7. Upload it, then add headline, description and Shop Now CTA, and check the link.

### Specifications, Ratios and Safe Areas

- Feed ad ratio is 4:5, which is 1080 x 1350 pixels. Reels, Stories and TikTok use 9:16 portrait. Placing a 9:16 edit into a feed placement crops it to 4:5, so text outside the 4:5 area can be cut off. [L0344.034]
- Change the project ratio with the ratio button next to the playhead. 9:16 is the usual portrait ratio for Reels, Stories and TikTok. Build the edit in 9:16 and keep all text and graphics within the 4:5 area so the feed crop does not remove them. [L0344.035]
- Placing text high on the frame, near the face, is a common mistake because feed placements crop the top and bottom of a 9:16 edit. Move the text down so it stays inside the 4:5 area. Use a 4:5 overlay or a locked guide layer while editing. [L0344.036]
- Blend modes for an overlay [L0344.037]:

| Blend mode | Effect |
|---|---|
| Screen | Removes dark areas and keeps light ones |
| Multiply | Keeps dark areas and removes light ones |
| Soft light | A softer version of these two |
| Darken | Keeps only dark areas |
| Brighten | Keeps only light areas |

  - Lower the overlay opacity if the effect is too strong. [L0344.037]
- Facebook crops portrait video to 4:5 in feed placements. Keep the main subject and captions near the centre of the frame and place captions higher than the bottom edge, so the crop does not cut them off. [L1423.017]
- Overlay banners at the very top of a video can be cropped out when Facebook shows the feed version at 4:5. Export a 4:5 feed version with sale banners inside the visible area, and use a 9:16 version for Stories and Reels where the top banner stays visible. [L1363.056]
- Ad image specification for link ads: exactly 1200 by 627 pixels, with no more than 20% of the image as text. Ads with more than 20% text on the image are rejected immediately. [L0641.007]
- Images with more than 20% text get the ad denied right away, so keep text on the image well under that threshold. Ads are also commonly denied in review, which is why a support ticket is the next step. [L0641.008]
- Keep text on Facebook ad images under 20% of the image area (older, 2021-22). Check with a grid overlay before launch. Images that pass this rule have the best chance of approval. [L0799.008]
- (Older, 2021-22.) Facebook ad images should be JPG or PNG. Use square 1:1 for feed and in-stream video placements, and fullscreen vertical 9:16 for Stories. Use an image with little or no overlaid text to maximise delivery. [L1342.031]
- (Older, 2021-22.) In Ads Manager, Automatically Crop to Square creates a square version of an uploaded image for placements that need it. Crop Image lets you adjust the crop manually for each placement. [L1342.049]
- A square ad uses a 1080 x 1080 pixel frame, a 1:1 aspect ratio. [L1427.020]
- Set the frame size in the sequence settings of your editor, entering the horizontal and vertical pixel values for the ad format. Premiere Pro, DaVinci Resolve and other editors have an equivalent setting. [L1427.019]
- When Meta's ad preview shows dimension warnings for a 4:5 video, the warnings can be ignored in most cases, because a 4:5 file is served to most placements. Check that the upload shows the dimensions as fine, then continue. [L0647.035]

### Placements, Previews and Destinations

- Preview each ad on Instagram, the Facebook feed and Facebook Marketplace before publishing. The same carousel or picture can look different in each placement. [L0642.053]
- Separate formats for placements (procedure) [L0773.030]:
  1. Upload the horizontal video.
  2. Click Edit video, then Edit placements.
  3. Upload the story-format version for Stories.
  - Using the same video in two formats (feed and story) is also acceptable. [L0773.030]
- For a video that runs on Facebook and Instagram in different formats, upload the horizontal version first. Then click Edit video > Edit placements and upload the story version. Feed placements on Facebook and Instagram were the best placements for the lesson's account, so switch from automatic to manual placement once data shows feeds perform best. [L0774.038]
- (Older, 2021-22.) The same image, headline and body copy can run on Instagram news feed placements. Keeping one creative across Facebook and Instagram supports consistent recognition. [L0415.007]
- (Older, 2021-22.) Facebook creates the Instagram version of a Facebook feed ad automatically, so a separate Instagram ad does not have to be built. The Instagram version is identical to the Facebook version unless you change it. [L1169.011]
- (Older, 2021-22.) Facebook feed ads can use a short or long copy version. Long copy gives more room for product detail, and a View more link lets readers expand it, so the most important text should sit at the top. [L1169.012]
- (Older, 2021-22.) On Instagram, only the first line of body copy shows before the more link. The opening line must summarise what the ad is about so the viewer clicks to read the rest. [L1398.008]
- Set the ad destination to the product page or landing page for one-product stores. Use a collection link only when customers should choose between several products, such as fashion or jewellery. [L0648.027]
- Set the ad CTA to Shop Now when the ad sends traffic to a collection page. Use Shop Now with the product page link when the ad sends traffic to one product. [L0642.024]
- Multi-advertiser ads is on by default in the ad format section. Meta can personalise the ad and resize the creative for each viewer. Check the creative in the preview because it can be resized. [L0865.061]
- Ad-level Languages can translate the ad text based on the viewer's location or default language. It is optional and can stay off in a standard sales setup. [L0865.062]

### Meta Setup Settings

- Under Ad creative setup, Meta allows up to five Primary Text versions (shown as "Primary text (1 of 5)") and several headlines, added with the Add text option and Add headline option. When more than one text is entered, Meta shows the version it predicts will perform best. The label reads "Not optimised" while that setting is off. [L0348.028]
- The Set up test option under the creative section compares up to 7 different versions of one creative in a single test. It is meant to make sure new test ads receive delivery. [L0348.029]
- Advantage+ creative enhancements: the default is 6 of 8 enabled. Switch to All optimisations to turn all 8 on, which shows 8 out of 8 after saving. [L1029.021]
- Dynamic creative testing (DCT) combines many images with copy and headlines, and Facebook finds the best combinations. Adding a DCT ad as a new asset in a scaling CBO shows whether new creatives get spend. If it gets almost none, the creatives are likely not good enough. [L1107.023]
- Dynamic creative is being phased out by Meta. While it is still available, using it switches on the Flexible ad format, so the Flexible option is turned off in this test setup. [L1193.017]
- Some advertisers build creatives as dark posts, which are unpublished posts that appear only inside ads and not on the Page timeline. The speaker still preferred this older method for setting up his ads. [L0643.061]
- Promotion and sandbox ads run as dark posts and never appear on the Facebook page. Post publicly only the content you want public, to the page first. [L0649.040]
- Ad setup procedure for a creative test [L1193.018]:
  1. Name the ad with today's date, product name, CBO, ad set name and the ad number, following the naming formula.
  2. Choose Manual setup.
  3. Select the Facebook Page and the Instagram account that should appear as the ad identity.
  4. Choose the single image or video format and turn the Flexible option off.
  5. Open the Media box, choose Videos, and upload all the video creatives you have collected. Uploading takes time, so wait for it to finish.
  6. Paste the Primary Text and headlines, add one description, paste the landing page as the website URL, and select Shop Now as the CTA.
  7. Duplicate the ad once for each creative so every creative has its own ad, then publish all ads.
  [L1193.018]
- Creating the other two creatives in one ad set [L0647.010]:
  1. Click the three toggle buttons on the first ad, choose Duplicate, and repeat once more so there are three copies in total.
  2. Rename the copies Video 2 and Video 3.
  3. In each copy, open Ad creative, click Edit media, click Change video and select the next video (image ads also work).
  4. Keep the Primary Text, headline, description and call to action identical to the first ad.
  [L0647.010]

### Creative Testing Structure

- Andromeda retrieval engine (deployed December 2024): creative now does the audience targeting, so tight audience restrictions can hurt performance. Campaigns need genuinely different creative concepts, since small changes such as a word or a colour do not count. Campaigns with 8-15 truly different concepts are expected to outperform those with 2-3 variants of one idea. [L0613.044]
- A concept pairs one avatar with one angle: a specific pain point, a variation in messaging, or a demographic segment such as ethnicity, age group or life stage. [L1019.006]
- Test a new creative with the best existing audience, such as a winning interest or stacked interests, so only the creative varies. Reduce all other variables, including the offer and audience, to keep the test readable. [L0646.043]
- Moving a winning ad into a new ad set and forcing spend on it, while other creatives get almost no spend, can raise CPM and CPC. Test untested creatives in a separate ad set so Facebook can learn which one performs, instead of pushing one ad. [L0645.016]
- Ads that get almost no spend in a campaign with a clear winner usually have weaker results in Facebook's data, so this is normal. Test them separately in their own ad set before deciding they are dead. [L0645.017]
- Putting several ads into one ad set makes Meta concentrate spend on one or two ads while others get only cents. Creative test results are then unreliable, so use one ad per ad set during creative testing. [L1316.011]
- Creative testing structure with ABO [L1316.012]:
  1. Create one new campaign with budget set at ad set level (ABO).
  2. Create nine ad sets, each with a daily budget of 5 USD.
  3. Put exactly one new ad into each ad set.
  4. Target a winning interest in some ad sets and a broad audience in others.
  5. Let the campaign run and compare the nine ads on performance, then keep the best one.
- Creative testing with proven audiences [L0648.060]:
  1. Build one creative testing campaign whose ad sets are your winning audiences, one ad set per winning interest or custom audience.
  2. Add one new creative as three variations that differ only in the first 3-5 seconds (the hook).
  3. Keep the same three variations in every ad set so the results can be compared across audiences.
  4. Treat a creative as a likely winner only if it has the best CTR in all winning ad sets, not just one.
  5. Replace the older creative in the main campaigns with the confirmed winner.
- If one creative wins across all ad sets, use only that creative for scaling. If no creative wins everywhere, let the audiences decide: different creatives may be relatable to different audiences, so give the campaign several strong options. [L1177.045]
- Ad structure inside each ad set: one concept with its three hook variations, so each ad set holds three ads and every ad set carries the same three ads. Two ads per ad set is the minimum. Two to three is the recommended structure, and three to four are also possible. [L0902.021]
- Let each creative test run for two to three days before judging it. Judge the concept by profitability, and judge the hooks by hook rate. [L0902.022]
- Avatar testing procedure [L0906.018]:
  1. Keep the problem concept fixed, and choose one audience to test it on.
  2. Create several avatar variants of the same audience, for example by ethnicity, language or gender, such as English-speaking, Spanish-speaking, Latino, or Spanish-speaking men and women.
  3. Test each avatar version with the same problem concept.
  4. Keep the version that performs best and build more variants from it.
- Test several creative formats at once, such as carousel, meme-style image, slideshow and single picture ads. Compare their CTR and spend, and keep the format with the strongest CTR. Scale that one once it has enough spend. [L0650.057]
- Testing multiple creatives is effective. Changing the pictures and adding the offer inside the image improved results for an ad set that had been showing the same creative for a long time. [L0650.017]
- The Sandbox is a separate campaign for split-testing creatives. CBO tends to concentrate spend on the early winner, so new ads in core campaigns rarely get a fair test. [L0649.025]
- Launch the Sandbox only after the core campaigns are live. Test new creatives against the best-performing cold audience. When a new ad wins, move it into the core campaign and turn off the old one. [L0649.026]
- Launch a new campaign with a meaningful number of creatives, ideally between 10 and 20. Use the time before launch to produce as many creatives as possible while saving cash. [L1225.054]
- A single ad set with about 13 creatives, including four videos and nine static images, across different angles is a reasonable amount. Diversify angles, such as by gender, and add variants over time. [L1225.061]
- Beginners should aim to launch more than 20 ads per month. Use a monthly ad spend divided by 500 to 1,000 USD as a rough check of that number. [L1235.016]
- Creative and conversion matter more than the ad budget at the start. Fix the page and the creative first, then increase spend so the test produces useful data. [L1016.070]
- Creative quality and offer quality have a bigger effect on ROAS and scalability than the choice of scaling strategy. A strong creative with a good offer and a strong sales page makes the audience and budget structure matter less. [L0999.033]

### Creative Fatigue and Refresh

- Update and test creative ad variations weekly, and refresh campaign creatives every week. [L0238.010]
- Ads that get almost no spend in a campaign with a clear winner should be tested in their own ad set before being judged dead. [L0645.017]
- Do not copy a competitor's full creative. Meta can detect duplicates and penalise your ad when the competitor is penalised. Reuse only the proven hook (about the first 1-5 seconds), then change slides and clips, add your logo and product name. Alternatively use custom or UGC content when budget allows. [L0637.015]
- Meta's algorithm updates, such as Andromeda, are increasingly wary of AI-generated ads that look like spam. Generic or mass-produced ads can lose reach, so AI output must meet a real quality standard before going live. [L0498.003]
- A Dynamic Creative ad that gets almost no spend in a scaling CBO means the creatives are likely not good enough. Replace them rather than adding more of the same. [L1107.023]
- Use the Sandbox to bring new creatives into core campaigns when they win, and turn off the old one. [L0649.026]

### AI-Generated Creative

- Meta's algorithm may penalise or raise CPMs on AI-generated bottom-of-funnel ads uploaded directly, because it prefers original content and reads image metadata. If you make ads with ChatGPT, Claude or similar tools, upload the image to Canva and download it again before uploading to Meta. [L0316.011]
- The AI creative approach taught in this series applies across product categories, including pet, fashion and pain relief. The principles are not tied to one niche, so an advertiser can adapt them to any product. [L0498.005]
- A default AI answer, asked which resources it would study to build a system for effective Meta ad creative, relies mainly on Meta's own guidance plus generic inputs such as reviews, the product itself, competitors and ROAS. Generic sources like this do not reveal what actually works, which usually comes from testing over years. [L1478.006]
- Generic AI-made ads can lose reach on Meta; the quality bar in [L0498.003] applies to every AI output before it goes live. [L0498.003]

#### Further details

- Image and video ads should be placed in separate ad sets; when they share an ad set, the algorithm may stop delivering the image and push all budget to the video. Duplicate the ad set and place the winning image in its own ad set to test it. [L0642.018]

## Bottom-of-Funnel and Cold Video Campaign Mix

### Bottom-of-Funnel Ads: When They Work

- Bottom-of-funnel (BOF) ads work for products whose audience already understands the category and can buy without explanation, such as phone cases, water bottles, posture belts and validated books. These products skip the education phase and go straight to conversion. [L0315.004, L0316.001]
- Before you launch BOF, run this test: **"Can this product sell without explanation?"** If yes, launch BOF. If no, warm the audience first with educational video ads, so the pool is large enough before BOF retargeting starts. [L0315.015, L0315.004, L0316.001]
- If your product needs to solve an unfamiliar problem, warm cold audiences with video first. More than 50% of viewers automatically join the BOF pool. [L0316.001, L0316.015]
- For books: if other brands have already run and validated the same book, BOF can work, because the audience is already solution-aware. If the book is niche or new, warm it with video first. If competitors have already built awareness, BOF image ads can capture sales from their audience that did not convert. [L0316.025]
- BOF is a harvesting channel, not a discovery channel. It shows the offer and benefits to people who already know the solution or have engaged with you. Because no spend goes toward explanation, CPMs stay low and ROAS rises. [L0316.002]
- Competitors' video ads can prepare buyers for you. If they have already warmed an audience to a solution, such as posture belts, and some people did not buy, your BOF image ads can capture those almost-converted customers. They did the education; you harvest the sale with a simpler, cheaper approach. [L0316.024]
- Example: one personal brand earned $100,000 in its first six months using only BOF image ads, starting from zero. The product was easy to understand (not a niche supplement) and the audience was solution-aware, so no warm-up video campaign was needed yet. [L0316.003]

### Writing BOF Creative

- BOF ads should read like direct sales ads: simple, straightforward and selling immediately. Tell the customer what they are buying and why, then ask them to buy. Skip storytelling and narrative, which is the opposite of educational ads. [L0316.040]
- For wearables and health products, BOF ads succeed by addressing emotional transformation, not technical specifications. Cold buyers respond to "you can have your life back" more than to "compression ratio of 360 degrees." Testimonials, pain-point questions and risk removal all convert cold audiences. [L0313.022]
- Guarantees work in BOF because they remove the main objection of cold traffic: "I don't know if this works." [L0313.025]
  - Specific terms are more credible than open-ended guarantees, for example 30 nights or a 7-day benefit window. [L0313.025]
  - The trial period must be long enough to test (30+ days). The benefit timeline must be realistic: 7 days for pain relief, not 1 day. [L0313.025]
- Pattern: BOF targets people who already know the product and are close to buying. It converts with a direct, number-based offer and a clear reason to act now, because that removes the last reason to hesitate. Formula: aware audience + specific number-based offer + clear reason to act now. [L0794.030]
  - "Still comparing? Take 35% off your first order this week." [L0794.030]
  - "Your size is still in stock: 20% off when you add the second copy." [L0794.030]
- Meta's algorithm assigns different concepts to different customer types based on scrolling behaviour and purchase history. Skeptics see testimonials, procrastinators see urgency and fence-sitters see value offers. You do not need to segment manually. Create several concepts in separate ad sets and let Meta optimise. [L0316.026]
- Static hot ads do not link to product pages. They build trust and authority with customer voices and link to the store. They complement the dynamic product ads that run alongside them. [L0649.021]
- Advertorial retargeting: visitors who read an advertorial but did not click through to the product can be retargeted with Meta ads. This is a more qualified audience than cold traffic. It works best as a step in a warm-up path for lower-ticket products, before building a longer funnel for expensive items. [L0552.050]
- Cold Facebook traffic should go to an editorial page, not directly to the product page. The editorial page's call-to-action button links to the product page. Facebook ads are good at catching impulse buyers, but sending all traffic to a product page leaves most of the prospects unconverted. [L0578.006, L0578.033]

### Campaign Structure and Separation

- Keep BOF as a separate campaign from cold video campaigns. [L0316.004, L0315.001]
- Within one BOF campaign, create one ad set per concept: testimonial, free gift, benefit, social proof and urgency. Each ad set targets the same warm audience from a different psychological angle. [L0315.001, L0316.004]
- Put 4 to 8 ad variations with different hooks in each ad set. Meta measures which concept converts best and spreads budget across ad sets so no single concept dominates spend. [L0315.002, L0316.004]
- Use separate Campaign Budget Optimization (CBO) campaigns for each format:
  - one for cold video
  - one for BOF (warm)
  - one for native ads, from week 2 onward, if used [L0316.014]
- Never mix formats in one CBO. If a high-performing video campaign shares a CBO with BOF ads, the BOF ads get no budget and no fair test. [L0316.014]
- Put the BOF statics and videos for the same product in different campaigns. Video ads in the same campaign can starve the statics, which then die. [L1476.036]
- Grouping, which differs from the rule above: when deciding campaign structure, group native and BOF ads together as **statics** and keep video ads in a separate campaign. [L1476.059]
  - Conflict: L0316.014 says one CBO per format, including native. L1476.059 says native and BOF share a static campaign. Both are valid structures. Use one CBO per format to keep each format's budget separate. Use the statics grouping when you want native and BOF to compete for the same static budget. [L0316.014, L1476.059]
- ABO or CBO matters less than having ads that actually win. Separate campaigns per format let you control budget and scale each format on its own. They do not guarantee better results. [L1476.058]

### Budgets and Test Length

- Starting budget for BOF testing: $50 USD per day. Minimum viable test: $15-20 AUD (about $15 USD) per day. Lower budgets often give higher ROAS, because the algorithm has more time to find your best audience. [L0316.012]
- Test BOF for 2 to 3 days. If it is not profitable by day 3, stop. Try new concepts or give up on this approach. Do not keep spending on an unprofitable test beyond this window. [L0316.013, L0316.031]
- Starter setup for a product: [L0316.034]
  - Kalodata rips at $50/day (priority; validates the product)
  - BOF image ads at $50/day (side test; low cost; quick results)
  - Both in separate CBOs for 2 to 3 days
  - Native ads come in week 2, if profitable
  - This spreads risk and tests three angles at once.
- If your Kalodata rip campaign is not profitable after 2 to 3 days, test BOF image ads instead of pausing. The audience may be solution-aware and tired of competitors' videos but has not bought yet. BOF is faster and cheaper to produce than video and can capture warm-but-not-buying segments. [L0316.047]
- Scaling limit: when you scale to $200/day and reach breakeven (1.0X ROAS), do not raise the budget further. The constraint is the ads, not the budget. Test new formats (BOF, native, advertorials, listicles), new concepts or AI UGC. The biggest jumps come from new creative that works, not from scaling mediocre ads. [L0316.037]

### Ad Set Rotation and Creative Fatigue

- Typical BOF structure on an active brand: 30+ ad sets in total, with 15 to 20 active at any moment. [L0316.041, L0316.048]
- Introduce new concepts every 2 days. Keep winners and pause losers. [L0316.041]
- Pause fatigued ad sets rather than deleting them. Do not delete old ad sets; disable them and retest later if needed. [L0316.041, L0316.048]
- As new warm audiences enter your pool from video campaigns, retest disabled sets to see whether they perform better on the fresh audience. [L0316.048]
- Procedure for a new BOF campaign: [L0316.031]
  1. Launch a separate CBO campaign for BOF (not in the same CBO as videos).
  2. Create three starting ad sets: testimonial, free gift, benefit.
  3. Add 4 to 8 ads to each ad set.
  4. Set the daily budget to $50 (or a minimum of $15-20 AUD).
  5. Run for 2 to 3 days.
  6. If ROAS is profitable, keep running and introduce new concept variations weekly. If it is not profitable by day 3, stop and test new concepts or different products.
- Ad type: BOF image ads perform as well as video ads at capturing warm, solution-aware audiences. Images are faster and cheaper (about 20 minutes with AI prompts). Videos take longer. Use images to test quickly and introduce video concepts once images prove the market. [L0316.042]

### How Video and BOF Work Together

- Every viewer who watches 50% or more of a video is automatically added to a warm audience pool. These viewers then move into the BOF campaign, which grows the pool of buyers for your image ads without extra work. [L0316.015]
- BOF profitability compounds as the warm pool grows. A larger pool gives BOF more impressions at low CPMs, which raises ROAS. Sustained cold spending increases BOF returns without extra effort, creating a self-reinforcing cycle. [L0315.012]
- Video builds the audience and BOF harvests it. BOF is a harvesting channel with a hard ceiling, typically $80K-$100K monthly revenue. The real scale comes from video ads building a larger warm pool, with BOF monetising the result. Together they can produce 5X or more revenue growth. [L0316.017]
- Example (personal brand): video campaigns were introduced at scale alongside existing BOF ads. ROAS doubled and budget scaled from $300 per day to $1,000 per day using the same BOF ads, with no new creative. The video campaign warmed the audience and BOF captured the results. [L0316.016]
- Example (Brand A): the brand hit an $80,000 monthly revenue ceiling running only BOF ads. When video ads at scale were added alongside the same BOF campaign, monthly revenue rose 5X to $400,000+. Video prospected cold audiences and warmed them, and BOF captured the ready-to-buy segment. [L0316.018]
- A high number of BOF ads for a product signals existing demand, because those ads target warm audiences who already know the product. It is a sign the product is easier to sell. [L1187.017]

### Funnel-Stage Creative Mix

- Top-of-funnel (TOF) ads educate cold audiences who do not yet know the product. They simply show what it is. [L1187.018]
- A healthy product ad set includes a mix of top-, middle- and bottom-funnel ads, not only one stage. [L1187.018]
