- Typical length: BOF product ads run about 1 to 10 seconds. A much longer ad deserves a closer look before you assume it is BOF. [L1187.019]
- Skip a product if almost all its ads are BOF with no top- or middle-funnel variety. It gives too little variety to test with. [L1187.020]
- Inside one CBO, the mix is heavily BOF: about 90% of ads at that stage, with the rest at middle and top. Plan creatives around that mix. [L1195.056]

### Native Ads

- Native ads are ads with a long primary text that reads like an article. They are a strong top-of-funnel format. Spot them by the long primary text and copy their structure. [L1164.032]
- Native ads scale less than UGC or BOF ads, so run them as a side campaign for extra revenue. Start them only after a winning product is confirmed and the pixel is warmed up, and only with budget set aside for testing. [L1476.055]
- Timing: native ads run from week 2 onward, in their own CBO, if the product is profitable. [L0316.014, L0316.034]

### Whitelist and Persona Pages

#### Concepts

- A Facebook Page is the public identity above an ad: a page name, a profile picture and posts. For the customer, it is the face that speaks to them, so the page you run an ad from shapes how it is perceived. [L1474.001]
- A brand page is the official Facebook page of your store or product brand, usually carrying the store name. Ads from it show the company speaking directly about its product. [L1474.002]
- A whitelist page is another Facebook page or creator identity that has given permission for ads to run under its name. The product stays the same, but a different person or page appears as the speaker. Meta calls this arrangement Community Handled Partnership Ads and manages permissions through its partnership tools. [L1474.003]
- Two whitelisting types: [L0906.013]
  - Personal whitelisting uses a creator, influencer or advocate persona that the audience trusts as a peer.
  - Third-party whitelisting uses an authority figure, such as a doctor for supplements, to lend credibility.
- Whitelist ads are usually written in the first person, from a named persona describing their use of the product. Some use a third-person page voice that describes the product. Both differ from the brand page's company voice. [L1474.015]

#### Brand Page versus Whitelist Page

| | Brand page | Whitelist / partner page |
|---|---|---|
| Who is talking | Your brand | Another approved page or creator |
| Product being sold | Your product | Can be the exact same product |
| How it feels | The company is advertising | A person or page is recommending |
| Main benefit | Builds your brand | Gives another angle or identity to test |

[L1474.007]

- Customers see ads all day, and an ad straight from a brand often reads as plain advertising. An ad from a creator or relevant page feels more like a recommendation and more organic, so the creative feels more native. [L1474.008]
- Whitelist pages are used to: [L1474.009]
  - test a different identity without changing the product
  - make creatives feel more native or personal
  - borrow a creator's credibility or style, once permission is granted
  - compare which page identity gets better results for the same product
- A brand page and a whitelist page can promote the same product at the same time. This lets you compare two identities side by side. [L1474.012]
- Procedure: [L1474.010]
  1. Start with the product you want to sell.
  2. Choose the face of the ad: your brand page or an approved partner page.
  3. Run the ad from the chosen page.
  4. Compare results across the pages.

#### Rules and Compliance

- Only run ads under another person's or business's identity when they have granted permission through Meta's partnership or advertising tools. Never pretend to be someone else, copy their page, or use their identity without consent. [L1474.006]
- An invented persona can be your own whitelist page, so it does not have to be an existing creator. An invented persona has no real access to your ad account and must be treated as a separate identity. [L1474.004]
- A whitelist identity may not use the name of a real celebrity. Meta rejects those ads and they are not approved. Use an invented name. [L1474.005]
- A whitelist page you build and own is presented as a fictional character instead of a company logo. The ads run under that character's identity. You keep the page, its audience and the pixel in your own account. [L1475.001]
- A character page is treated as a branded page with a personality, similar to a mascot brand. It stays compliant only if the character is clearly a brand persona. It must not imitate a real, identifiable person, and standard Meta ad policy still applies. Check Meta's current Page authenticity and ad policies before scaling, since enforcement can change. [L1475.002]
- Mistake: using a real person's name, photo or likeness for the character can get the page or its ads rejected or disabled. Keep the persona fully fictional, with an AI-generated or licensed stock portrait. [L1475.003]
- Invented personas are often shown with stock photos as profile pictures. A stock photo does not change the permission and compliance rules for whitelist pages. [L1474.016]
- Checking a page before you treat it as a possible identity: open its Facebook page view and check the Details section, the Links and Contact info fields, the follower count and the Photos section. The page view also shows a "Not yet rated" or review status, which gives a quick sense of whether the identity looks like a real, active page. [L1474.020]

#### Building the Page

- Procedure, character assets built with AI: [L1476.017]
  1. Attach your customer research and product documents to Claude.
  2. Paste the character-generator prompt.
  3. Let Claude read the research, pick the archetype and generate the character profile and assets.
  4. Copy the profile photo prompt, the cover photo prompt and the bio.
  5. Paste the two image prompts into ChatGPT to generate the images.
  6. Upload the images and the bio to the new Facebook page.
- Procedure, creating the page in Facebook Business Manager: [L1476.019]
  1. Create a new Facebook page inside Business Manager.
  2. Upload the profile photo.
  3. Upload the cover photo.
  4. Upload the bio.
  5. Start running ads from that page.
- Setup is the same as a brand page: create the page, upload profile photo, cover photo and bio, then run ads from it. The AI prompts do most of the character work, so this is beginner-friendly. [L1476.034]
- One whitelist character can run all of a brand's ads without extra impersonation risk. A different page per person shown in UGC is also possible, but each character must not imitate a real person. [L1476.035]

#### When to Use Whitelist Pages

- Start with a whitelist page from day one of testing, rather than waiting for a winning ad. This is recommended on a tight budget, unless brand pages have already proven better for the product. [L1476.020]
- Make the whitelist page the default for the whole ad account, rather than running brand pages. A face instead of a logo means more people watch, fewer scroll past and more click, and this adds up at scale. [L1476.026]
- Whitelist pages suit static ads well, since a static image without a face needs only the character page. UGC videos without faces can also use the whitelist page with no extra work. Video ads with a face need the matching-look consideration. [L1476.030]
- Split test whitelist against brand page: run the same five ads once with each page. This splits the budget in half, and both tests need identical ads for a fair comparison. With a small budget and few ad sets, run only the whitelist page. [L1476.021]
- If only a few ads are tested on a small budget, use the whitelist page for all of them and drop the brand page. This avoids splitting the budget across two pages. [L1476.022]

### Researching Competitor Pages

- To see how competitors run whitelisting or persona pages: [L0906.011]
  1. Open the Meta Ad Library at facebook.com/ads/library.
  2. Set active status to Active.
  3. Set ad type to All ads.
  4. Set country to All.
  5. Set search type to Page.
  6. Sort by total impressions, descending, to see which pages run the most ads.
  7. Search by the competitor's page name, not by a product keyword.

## Competitor Research and the Meta Ad Library

### Opening the Ad Library and searching

- The Meta Ad Library is a free, public Meta tool that lists the active ads of any Facebook or Instagram page, including video and image ads, sorted from newest to oldest by start date. Use it to study competitor creatives, offers and how long each ad has run. [L0667.009, L0675.007, L1008.001]
- It is the public archive of Facebook and Instagram ads. Combined with the filters of a brand tool, you can search it by country, language, niche, media type, days running, page active ads and ad creation date. [L0374.039]
- Anyone can see every running ad, and sellers cannot remove their ads from it, so it shows competitors that third-party tools may not show or may hide. [L0335.046]
- The library shows which ads run, their run dates and their creative angles. It does not show sales results, and some ads show no shop button or product details. Use it to study hooks, formats and angles, not as proof that a product sells. [L0552.051, L0549.018]
- It does not show likes, comments or shares of an ad. For engagement, check the advertiser's posts for high like counts or use a spy tool that tracks an ad over time. [L1108.016]
- For most ads the public library does not show CTR or spend, so filters by high CTR or high spend may not exist there. Judge candidates by visible signals such as engagement, comments and run length, and verify which data fields the library actually displays before filtering. [L1252.025]
- It does not show which countries an advertiser targets. Use other tools or store and ad data to find target countries. [L1176.012]
- Each ad card shows a Library ID, the platforms it runs on, the page name, the date it started, its running time and a line such as "3 ads use this creative and text". [L1474.013, L1474.018, L1249.019]

#### Basic search procedure

1. Open facebook.com/ads/library (or search "Facebook Ads Library"). [L0204.010]
2. Select "All ads" from the dropdown menu. [L0204.010]
3. Search for the competitor brand name or page name. [L0204.010]
4. If the page name does not appear in the dropdown, paste the competitor website URL instead. [L0204.010]
5. Click "View ads" to display all active or recent competitor ads. [L0204.010]
6. Record ad copy and creative patterns that repeat across 2 or more campaigns, since repetition indicates performance. [L0204.010, L0204.011]
7. Note the headline, primary text structure and opening hooks the competitor uses consistently. [L0204.010]

#### Filter settings

- Set Active status to Active ads so only currently running campaigns appear. [L1249.018]
- Set the Language filter to English (US) so results come from the English-speaking market you target. [L1249.018]
- Set the country to United States and the category to All ads when researching a US competitor. [L0855.180]
- The Active status filter is set to Active by default. Ads marked Inactive appear with that label. Set the filter to see only running ads, then read the Running Xd label on each card. [L1474.019]
- Standard search settings: Active status Active; Ad type All ads; Country ALL; Media type All. Enter the product or niche keyword in the search field. [L1474.014]
- Use Show EU data for EU transparency information on an ad. Some ads carry a note that they are not active for some audiences because they did not meet certain requirements. [L1474.017]
- Use Save on a keyword search to return to the same list of competitor stores later. [L1249.020]

#### Keyword searches for products

- Enter one keyword at a time. Multiple keywords joined together are not supported. [L0678.018, L0678.019]
- Search word variations, such as singular and plural forms and synonyms (for example shoe, shoes, sneaker, sneakers), since different ads use different wording. [L0678.018, L0678.019]
- Filter by active ads, then review the results to find products being advertised. [L0678.019]
- A keyword search counts every ad that matches the word. The result number is broad and is not the exact count of one product's ads. [L0707.004]

#### Verifying a tool's numbers

1. Note the product's active ad count in the research tool. [L1185.005]
2. Open the same product in the Facebook Ads Library. [L1185.005]
3. Confirm that the listed ads are marked active and that the number matches. [L1185.005]
4. Check which countries the ads run in. [L1185.005]
5. Check the start date of the newest ad to confirm the product is still being tested or scaled. [L1185.005]

#### Verifying demand for a product

1. Open the Meta Ad Library and search the product keyword or the brand's page name. [L1479.011]
2. Set Active status to Active ads and the country to United States. [L1479.011]
3. Check the Platforms icons to see where each ad runs. [L1479.011]
4. Note the Started running on date and the Library ID of each ad. Ads running for weeks or months are the strongest signal. [L1479.011]
5. Count the brand's active ads, then scroll to the bottom to see older tests. [L1479.011]
6. Open See ad details to read the full copy, the creative and the landing page link. [L1479.011]

### Winning Hunter filters for the Meta Ad Library

- Winning Hunter's Meta Ad Library is the platform's main feature, labelled Meta Ads Library (recommended). Unlike ad spy tools that rank by likes and comments, it shows the ads themselves, so use it for ad research. [L0892.022]
- Filters, in order: Countries, Language, Video length (0s to 3600s), Niche, Media type, Page type, Apps, Themes, Reach (Views), Ad copy length (characters, 0 to 50000), Low Impressions, Days Running (0 to 365), Product Price Range (0 to 5000), Store Traffic (Monthly Visits), Ad creation date, Page Active Ads (0 to 10000), Last seen date, Product/Collection Creation Date and Page creation date. [L0892.023]
- Further options: Sort By (Updated or Last Seen), Dropship Ads, Advertorials, Native ads, Quiz Funnels, Supplements, Top Branded, EU adspend, Save current filters and Reset. Use Save current filters for searches you run often. [L0892.024]
- Themes and Apps narrow pages by Shopify theme (for example Dawn, Impulse or Impact) and by installed apps. Checkout integration filters such as Checkout Champ or Lasso/Whop checkout find stores that run a particular checkout provider. [L0892.036]
- A brand page has source switches for Meta Ads, Ad Library and Facebook Post Ads, plus a Global Ads or EU/UK Only toggle. Use EU/UK Only when the planned market is Europe or the UK, so regional ads are not mixed with global ones. [L0892.038]

#### Filter minimums

| Filter | Value | Purpose | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Days running | at least 7 days | Removes short tests | [L0374.038] |
| Page active ads | at least 200 | Shows pages with scale | [L0374.038] |
| Ad creation date | within the last 90 days | Shows ads still working | [L0374.038] |
| Page Active Ads | minimum 80 | Finds large established competitors; the Checkout Champ filter returns fewer results with the same settings | [L0892.026] |
| Video length | set a range | Finds VSLs (video sales letters) | [L0892.027] |
| Ad copy length | set characters | Finds native ads | [L0892.027] |

#### Finding newly launched Shopify pages

1. Open the Page creation date filter and set the range to the last 30 days. [L0892.025]
2. Add the Shopify platform and the checkout filters you need, such as Apps or Themes. [L0892.025]
3. Sort by Last Seen and review the new pages that run ads. [L0892.025]

#### Reading ad cards and detail views

- An ad card shows EST. SPEND, REACH, number of countries, active ads for the page and Est. Product Revenue. [L0892.029]
- The ad detail view adds running days, duplicates, format, gender, age range, first seen, last seen, landing page and estimated monthly traffic. [L0892.029]
- Quick actions: Find Competitors with, Visit Store, Page Ad Library, Download Video, Track Store, Fulfill with Ecomm..., Download Thumbnail and Transcribe ad with.... They move you from an ad to the store, its library page or its files in one step. [L0892.030]

### Tracking a competitor brand

#### Winning Hunter brand tracking procedure

1. Copy the brand name or page name from the competitor's ad. [L0772.027]
2. Open Winning Hunter Brands, search for the name and click Follow. [L0772.027]
3. On the brand's page, use the View Page or Ad Library link to open the brand's Meta Ad Library page and note the total results (130 in the example). [L0772.027]
4. Return to Brands and paste the Meta Ad Library link into the add-brand field so the tracker can gather the brand's live ads. [L0772.027]
5. Wait for the data to fill in, then review the brand's top hooks, angles and landing pages. [L0772.027]

#### Brand tracker details

- The Brands list shows each tracked brand's media mix as counts and percentages for video and image ads. It shows at a glance whether a competitor relies on video or static creatives. [L0772.042]
- On a brand's detail page, the Global Ads and EU/UK Only switch limits the data to that scope. Visit Page opens the store page, Save saves the brand, and Ad Library opens its Meta Ad Library page. A Growth indicator shows whether the number of launched ads is Stable or changing. [L0772.043]
- Filter to active ads and read each ad's start date to judge how long it has run. A long run of active ads signals a working creative. [L0772.032]
- The Meta Ad Library, Pinterest Ads library and TikTok Ads library each show a competitor's active ads. Open the brand in Brand Tracker for its full ad set. [L1164.030]
- The Top Ads view ranks ads by ad rank with up to 10 placements. You can sort by Top Ad rank, Most Reach or Longest running. Rank #1 is the best, so a lower rank number means stronger performance. [L1164.031]
- On product cards, the number of active ads and the number of countries show how heavily a product is tested. A product with hundreds of active ads across dozens of countries has been tested far more than one with a handful, so start from the most heavily tested products. [L1164.053]

#### Count discrepancies

- The active-ad count in Winning Hunter can be lower than the total results on a Meta Ad Library page for the same advertiser. In the example, the library showed 130 results while Winning Hunter counted 124 active ads. Check both numbers when judging a competitor's scale. [L0772.046]
- A brand with around 130 active ads has likely validated its creatives and is a good reference. Use the ad count as a quick quality check. [L0772.033]

### Competitor research procedure

1. Open the competitor's page in the Meta Ad Library. [L0524.034]
2. Review the active ads and filter for video ads to see the dominant format. [L0524.034]
3. Note each ad's hook, the problem it names, the proof it uses and the offer. [L0524.034]
4. Group the ads by angle, such as education, identity, social proof or offer. [L0524.034]
5. Save the strongest ads to a swipe board for reference. [L0524.034]

- Use the Ad Library to see which ads competitors run, how long they have run and where the ads send people, such as an advertorial or a product page. [L1131.020]
- Look at ad copy and video or image together when judging a winner. The Ad Library shows both the product and the winning creative, so it can reveal which hook or angle to copy with the product. [L0638.021]
- Set a list of 15 to 25 potential products from the Ad Library, then rank them by your defined product criteria. [L0638.022]
- Test products one by one. Some are tested by only one or two sellers and are not yet covered by spy tools. [L0638.022]
- Set a starting age range with an ad spy tool:
  1. Look up the product in the ad spy tool. [L0678.043]
  2. Note the age range that competing advertisers target. [L0678.043]
  3. Use that range only as a reference point for your own test. [L0678.043]
  4. Keep the test broad, for example 18 to 65+, and narrow the age range only after sales data shows which group performs best. [L0678.043]

### Signals of a winner

#### Run time

| Run time | Reading | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Under two weeks | Weaker signal of success | [L0639.026] |
| 7 days or more | Filter floor that removes short tests | [L0374.038] |
| One month or more | Likely profitable and worth studying | [L0639.026] |
| Weeks or months | Stronger signal than a new ad; use as layout reference | [L0794.028, L1479.012] |
| Many months | Strongest signal; study these first | [L1164.033] |

- An ad that keeps running for weeks or months is likely profitable, because advertisers do not keep paying for ads that lose money. Use run length as proof that the creative and offer work. [L1479.012]
- Long-running ads show which creatives the advertiser keeps. Check run dates and active ad counts on each ad card. [L1164.033]

#### Duplicates and creative count

The thresholds below come from different lessons and describe different things. Apply each where it fits; they are not interchangeable.

| Count of ads or ad sets with the same creative and text | Reading | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Same copy in 2 or more campaigns at once | Copy is performing; the advertiser would not keep investing otherwise | [L0204.011] |
| 10 or more ads | Likely in the scaling phase; product probably works | [L0638.019] |
| 10 to 15 ads by one brand | Creative is usually being scaled | [L1382.051] |
| 13 or more ad sets with identical text | Clear winner; treat as proof rather than a guess | [L0638.020] |
| 15 or more ads | Winning creative; sellers do not repeat weak creatives this often | [L0638.019] |

- The Ad Library count reflects how many ad sets use a creative, because one ad set cannot run the same ad twice. The campaign structure is campaign, then ad set, then ad. The count of ad sets is the signal to read. [L0638.018]
- Sellers usually scale a single creative only after the product has become profitable. Many identical ads from one advertiser point to a proven winner. [L0895.029]
- Duplicates on an ad show how many other advertisers use the same creative. Several duplicates mean the product or angle is already being copied. [L0892.028]
- A large number of active ad variations for the same product, and the same video running repeatedly over a long period, is a strong sign of profit. Repeated runs show the advertiser keeps spending because it returns money. [L0707.005]

#### Active ad counts and scaling

- Track how many active ads a competitor runs over several days. A jump, for example from about 50 to about 100 active ads in one day, signals many new launches and scaling. A competitor usually scales only a profitable product, so many new ads on one product is a strong signal. [L0335.007]
- Count active ads per single creative, not the total on the page. A page can show around 500 active ads overall while one creative runs only about 31. Only the per-creative count shows real scaling. [L1177.055]
- A competitor who launches new ads every day is actively scaling, which is a stronger signal than a static number. Check the start dates of the newest ads to see whether launches continue. [L1177.056]
- A competitor starting many new ads in a short time often signals a funnel being tested. [L1131.020]
- In the 2025 example, a brand with about 71 active ads that started in early July was still testing. The main brand behind the same product had about 650 active ads and was clearly scaling. [L1479.013]
- Large page counts are a filter tool: 200 active ads minimum for a page to show scale, 80 minimum to find large established competitors. [L0374.038, L0892.026]

#### Page age

1. Search the page name, or the store URL if the name does not turn up the page. [L0335.006]
2. Open one of the page's ads, then go to the About section to read the creation date. [L0335.006]
3. Read the page creation date in the About section, then open Page transparency on the page to see its ad activity. [L1177.054]

- A page created within the last few months that already runs many ads signals a new competitor testing a trend. An old page with a warmed pixel and large purchase history gets better delivery and is harder to beat. [L0335.006]
- An older page is a stronger sign of success, but a new page with well-performing ads can still be worth testing. [L1176.014]
- A competitor whose page was created recently has not yet built a large purchaser audience in its pixel, so a new entrant starts on roughly equal footing. This is a useful plus but not a mandatory requirement. [L1183.019]

#### Still running

To check whether an ad is still running:

1. Open the ad in the Ad Library. [L0335.017]
2. Set the comments sort to Newest. [L0335.017]
3. Look for comments from the last 24 to 48 hours. If comments still arrive, the ad is probably running and likely profitable. [L0335.017]

- Check that a competitor's ad is active before deciding to test its product. [L1176.013]
- To check a competitor's ad activity, open facebook.com/ads/library, select All, then All ads, and search for the page name. The results show that page's active ads. [L1177.053]
- The Facebook Ad Library lists a competitor's active ads and run dates, which shows how a store is scaling on Facebook. [L0871.071]
- For a product, check three things before deciding whether to test it: the active ad count, the date the ads started and the countries they run in. [L1171.051]
- The Facebook Ad Library is the basis for counting how many ads a competitor runs for one product. [L1174.024]

#### Engagement

- Shares are the most useful engagement signal on a competitor's ad. Likes and comments can be bought cheaply, while shares are hard to fake. A product with a high share count is stronger proof of concept than one with many likes alone. [L0335.004]
- Many ads have comments turned off, so few comments or likes do not necessarily mean weak demand. Check sales data on AliExpress or other sources before judging a product by ad engagement. [L0706.023]

#### Spend and reach estimates

- Winning Hunter ad cards show an estimated spend and estimated reach for each ad. Read the two together to see which ads a competitor keeps funding, but treat both as estimates, not the advertiser's real spend. [L1164.052]

#### Market signals

- Competitor videos with sold-out messages, strong urgency or call-to-action text often indicate paid ads. Check the store page for ad-style elements to confirm. [L0352.052]
- When many competing stores run ads for the same product at once, do not assume the market is closed. A market can support several stores; only giant brands such as Apple or Amazon are real exceptions. [L1176.018]
- Political advertisers cannot switch ads off freely, so an active political ad does not prove profitability. Use active status and run duration as a profit signal only for ordinary, non-political ads. [L0639.045]

### Copying cautions

- Copying a competitor's ad exactly does not reproduce its results, because their pixel data, audience pocket and timing differ from yours. The variables you control are your creative and your landing page. [L0639.033]
- Competitors who seem to succeed with simple drop-shipping videos often have an older pixel with accumulated data, or they launched when competition for the product was low. Running the same video without that data or timing usually produces nothing. [L0639.034]
- Copying competitor ads and stores is a valid starting point for beginners but not a guaranteed success. If copying fails, change the angle (different problem, audience or context) rather than repeating the same creative. [L0640.035]
- If a copied ad or store does not sell, possible causes include the competitor having a longer-running pixel with more data, entering the market earlier, or an audience saturated with the product. The response is to test a new angle, not repeat the same copy. [L0640.052]
- Replicating winning creatives is a useful shortcut, and the winner can be adapted with a local voice-over or local captions for a new market. [L1382.051]
- Launch new concepts with the competitor's ad only as a shortcut, then move on to your own content. [L1345.083]

### Ripping creatives

#### Ripping images from long-running ads

1. Open the Meta Ad Library and search for the competitor page with the most active ads. [L0794.027]
2. Set Active status to Active ads. [L0794.027]
3. Set Media type to Images and memes. [L0794.027]
4. Set the Impressions by date range longer if older ads should be included, then Apply. [L0794.027]
5. Sort by total impressions. [L0794.027]
6. Open each ad and note its Started running on date. [L0794.027]
7. Right-click the image and save it to the desktop, or inspect the page and copy the image address when saving is not possible. [L0794.027]

#### Video ripping

- Upload downloaded videos directly to Meta Ads Manager without any editing. The same clean files can also be uploaded to TikTok Ads. [L1252.012]
- The time from ripping a video to launching the ad is about one to two hours. This speed is a main reason to use ripped creatives for early tests. [L1252.015]
- Facebook is stricter than TikTok about reused video. Replace copied videos with your own content as soon as a concept is proven, and use copied material only for short tests of one to two days. [L1345.083]

#### Choosing which layouts to copy

- Large established brands can run plain image ads and still sell, because brand recognition, customer lifetime value, referrals and organic demand do much of the work. A new store cannot rely on that. It should copy the structure of ads that perform, not the simplicity of big-brand ads. [L0794.029]
- Use long-running ads as layout references when building new creatives. [L0794.028]

### Choosing products from the research

1. Build a list of 15 to 25 potential products from the Ad Library. [L0638.022]
2. Rank the products by your defined product criteria. [L0638.022]
3. Check run length, active ad counts, creative counts and countries for each product using the signals above. [L0638.019, L0638.020, L1164.053, L0374.038]
4. Test the products one by one. [L0638.022]
5. Use the Ad Library's product, creative and copy data together when choosing the hook for each test. [L0638.021]

## Diagnosing Performance: Metrics, AIDA and Benchmarks

### Set Up the Metrics and Read the Funnel as AIDA

- Add hook rate, hold rate and conversion rate (CVR) as custom metrics so each stage of the ad can be checked from attention through purchase. [L0008.001]
- Set up the data with custom metrics and column types for both the snapshot and the funnel. [L0008.008]
- Read the funnel as an AIDA sequence, left to right: spend and cost per result first as a snapshot, then the funnel metrics. [L0652.011]
- AIDA stands for Attention, Interest, Desire and Action. Evaluate whether the creative captures attention, maintains interest, creates desire to buy and drives action, then use the data to see where the breakdown occurs. [L0008.003]
- The source labels this framework "ADA", but its four stages are the AIDA stages, applied to the entire ad funnel. [L0067.005]
- Map each stage to a metric:
  - **Attention:** hook rate, measured in the first 3 seconds of the ad. [L0067.005]
  - **Interest:** CTR, measured as people clicking through to the page. [L0067.005]
  - **Desire:** conversion rate, measured as people adding to cart and viewing the product. [L0067.005]
  - **Action:** overall return, measured as ROAS. [L0067.005]
- The Desire stage is also read through hold rate and secondary signals. [L0652.011]
- Read ad metrics alongside business KPIs. Ad metrics show how the ad performs, and business KPIs show whether the sale makes money. [L0875.029]
- The ad's only job is to drive traffic to the store. It is not responsible for sales. [L0642.010]
- Good ad metrics with no sales usually point to a store problem. Good sales with weak ad metrics point to the audience or the creatives. Diagnose this split before deciding to kill a product. [L1161.022]

### Attention: Hook Rate and Hold Rate

- Hook rate benchmarks: aim for 30% or higher, 40% is great, and 50%+ is excellent. [L0067.006]
- The hook must be relevant to the target audience, not just attention-grabbing for its own sake. [L0067.006]
- A hook rate of 45 to 60% is considered good. Below roughly 40 to 45% indicates a problem in the first seconds of the video, which is usually a creative issue. [L1215.008]
- Hook rate is the first measure to check on every ad. [L1215.008]
- Read hook rate at the ad level, not the ad set level. An ad set groups several creatives and hides which video is weak. [L1215.032]
- Hook rate and video retention are purely creative-driven signals. [L1215.012]
- A high hook rate does not guarantee sales if the hook does not match the product or audience. Example: a meme ad of someone falling over had a 60% hook rate and zero conversions, because viewers only wanted entertainment, not the product. [L0067.016]
- A very high hook rate with almost no sales means the creative stops attention but may attract viewers who will not buy. Check whether the audience matches likely buyers before changing the hook again, because a strong hook rate alone does not show audience quality. [L0652.015]
- A video is working if at least a quarter of its total views reach 50% of the video length. If fewer than that pass the halfway point, the creative is the likely problem. [L1215.010]
- For the 50% retention check, use the video views figure, not impressions. Take one quarter of the views as the benchmark count and see how many reached 50% of the video. [L1215.033]
- Video average play time is the average watch time per impression and includes replays, so it can exceed the exact video length. Compare it with the length shown in your editor. A short average on a longer video signals weak retention. [L1215.035]
- Example: if 432 plays reach the 15-second mark and 178 of those viewers reach 95% of the video, about 41% stay to the end. Cross-check such a figure against other retention metrics, because Meta's numbers do not always agree. [L1215.036]

**Creative diagnosis procedure**

1. Check the hook rate on each ad. If it is below the threshold, the first seconds are the problem. [L1215.011, L1215.008]
2. If the hook rate is fine, check how many viewers reach 50% of the video. [L1215.011]
3. Compare the average play time with the actual video length. If a 40-second video has an average play time of about 6 seconds, the creative is weak. [L1215.011]

### Interest: Click-Through Rate

- CTR target: above 2%. There is no hard rule, because it depends on product and industry. Some profitable brands have very low CTR because only highly qualified buyers click. [L0067.007]
- On Facebook, aim for a CTR of at least 1.5 to 2%. A CTR around 4% is exceptional. [L0562.003]
- On Facebook, CTR matters because the platform's job for the advertiser is to send quality traffic to the store. A low CTR means people scrolling past are not stopping for the ad. A high CTR means the ad earns the click. [L0562.004]
- On platforms other than Facebook, follow the data on CTR and check that it is good. Expect more effort to turn those clicks into quality leads. [L0562.011]
- Target outbound CTR for Facebook ads is at least 1.5%, meaning roughly 15 clicks per 1,000 impressions. A CTR of 0.63% is considered too low and signals the creative or product needs changing. [L1212.002]
- When a Facebook ad is running, first check CTR. If outbound CTR is at or above 1.5% (some sources cite 2 to 3% as good), keep the ad running and focus on improving the store offer, because the ad's only job is to send traffic. [L1212.001]
- Low CTR with a good hook rate usually points to the wrong audience, or to weak ad copy or thumbnail. [L1215.012]
- A CTR of 2 to 3% with no sales suggests the offer or landing page is the problem, not the ad hook. Check the page and the offer before rewriting the creative. [L0524.040]
- A high CTR with zero sales indicates a page builder mismatch. Switching teams (e.g., PageFly to Source F Team) often resolves the issue. [L0209.007]
- A high CTR does not guarantee sales, because clickbait creatives can attract clicks without educating or convincing buyers. Check the rest of the funnel when CTR is high but sales are low. [L1260.010]
- Retargeting example: a CTR of 16% means 16 out of every 100 people who see the ad click it, a strong signal that the right audience is seeing it. A high CTR with no sales therefore points away from the audience and toward the store. [L1083.009]
- On a winning product, CTR is usually around 2% or higher, with 1.5% as the minimum that still counts as good. A CTR below that on a winning product is a sign to review the ad creative before blaming the store. [L0774.051]
- If CTR is strong but there are no sales, test different offers instead of only changing ads. [L0519.026]

**CTR procedure (outbound)**

1. Open the ad account and set the reporting column to CTR (all) to see the average click-through rate. [L1212.004]
2. Compare the outbound CTR against the 1.5% target. [L1212.004]
3. If CTR is below 1.5%, replace the images or videos in the creatives. [L1212.004]
4. If CTR stays low after new creatives, test a different market or change the product. [L1212.004]

### Cost Signals: CPM, CPC and Ad Quality

- CPC is a derived metric, not an independent one. It is calculated from CPM multiplied by CTR, so high CPM with good CTR naturally results in high CPC. [L0298.025]
- Arithmetic check: CPC = CPM ÷ (1,000 × CTR). Example: a CPM of $10 at a 1% CTR gives a CPC of $1. [L0298.025, L1260.059]
- CTR formula: link clicks ÷ impressions × 100. Example: 20 clicks from 1,000 impressions give a CTR of 2%. [L1260.059]
- If CPM is high but profitability is achieved, focus first on proving the product works. CPM and CPC are secondary optimization opportunities. [L0298.026]
- Low CPM on Meta comes from engagement. Likes and comments signal to the platform that people enjoy the ad, which lowers cost. [L0691.011]
- A high bounce rate, where visitors leave the landing page within a second, signals a poor page and raises CPM. [L0691.011]
- CPM is not something that can be changed day to day. It follows from ad quality and the landing page. [L0691.011]
- Meta reads early engagement as a quality signal. When more of the first audience watches, comments or interacts, the ad can reach a wider pool, which tends to lower CPM. Weak creatives get fewer engagements and narrower reach, which raises cost. [L0637.060]
- A creative that delivers poor user experience is penalized in delivery, which shows up as higher CPMs. Weak creative costs money even when the audience is right. [L0398.003]
- Ad-level rankings: quality, engagement and conversion rankings are shown for each ad. [L0398.003, L0646.057]
- An above-average engagement ranking usually comes with lower CPM and cheaper clicks than competing ads, so strong creative can lower acquisition cost. [L0646.057]
- In Facebook Ads Manager, the Quality Ranking and Engagement Ranking columns show whether an ad is below average, average or above average. They signal how Meta rates ad quality and engagement. [L1260.006]
- Use the quality and engagement ranking columns as an additional signal when diagnosing. [L1259.008]
- Lower CPM is a goal, but it does not by itself mean better performance. Expensive CPM can come with higher-intent audiences, so judge the ad by CTR and CPC together. [L1260.007]
- Low CPM alone does not guarantee good performance, because clickbait ads can attract clicks without generating sales. [L1259.007]
- A low CPM with a high CTR means the ads are doing their job, so the problem is most likely on the website, not in the ad. [L1259.007]
- A good CPM and CTR show the ad reaches the right audience, so a sales gap points to the website. Look at add-to-cart and conversion rate before changing the creative. [L1225.064]
- CPM examples from testing: about 8 to 9 USD for one product and about 20 to 30 USD for another. A high CPM means fewer impressions and fewer people reached, so read it together with CTR and CPC, not alone. [L0502.046]
- New creatives typically start at CPM 35 to 40 and naturally decline to CPM 20 to 23 by day 4, sometimes lower by day 5 or later, as real engagement and social proof accumulate. This applies regardless of creative quality. [L0298.054]
- New ads in an early product test usually start with a high CPM. Engagement signals and a low landing-page bounce rate push CPM down over time, so the starting CPM is not a final verdict. [L0691.033]

**Relevance score**

- Facebook's relevance score works like Google's quality score: higher engagement lowers CPC. Unlike Google, Facebook counts all engagement, such as likes, shares, comments, page likes and website clicks. An ad with few interactions can score low even with some engagement. [L0510.033]
- Example: an ad with one comment and seven likes had a relevance score of 2.9. Adding page likes, website clicks, shares, post likes and more comments raises the score. [L0510.034]
- Relevance scores are specific to each audience, so the same ad can score differently for two audiences. [L0510.034]
- Facebook's Relevance Score plays the same role as Google's Quality Score. Every form of engagement counts toward it, so more engagement generally helps. [L1031.026]

### Desire and Action: Conversion, Return and Acquisition Cost

- Conversion rate target from Shopify metrics: 2 to 3%. For dropshipping, 4% or higher is good, because longer shipping delays lower conversion rate. [L0067.009]
- For stores with multiple traffic sources, measure conversion rate separately by source (Google Shopping, Facebook Remarketing, organic, etc.). Do not use the blended conversion rate from Shopify Analytics, which includes bot traffic and irrelevant visitors. [L0249.053] *(Older advice, 2022.)*
- Cost per purchase in the Meta section must be compared against the target cost per purchase. Values below target are profitable. Values above target turn red and signal that new ads are needed. [L1354.015]
- Example: if Meta cost per purchase rises to $140 while the target is $115, the cell turns red. The campaign is unprofitable at that level and new ads are needed. [L1354.030]
- Customer acquisition cost of about 10 to 15 USD per order was reached. Ten orders cost about 100 to 150 USD in ads. [L0492.050]
- CPC is critical because it determines what traffic costs before the website even gets a chance to convert. Even an excellent website cannot scale if traffic is too expensive, especially on slow days when buying intent is lower. [L1354.014]
- Cost per click can be higher if the product is profitable and overall ROAS is good. [L0067.008]
- In the first weeks, a Meta pixel is still learning and needs data before it finds real buyers. Poor early sales therefore do not prove that the offer or the store is wrong. [L1445.003]
- Early sales followed by a drop-off often point to creative fatigue or a weak offer. Test more creatives and offers, and aim for consistent profit so that a bad day still yields profit. [L0524.039]

### Frequency and Ad Fatigue

- To check frequency, open Ads Manager, customize the columns to add "Frequency", and review the figure over the past seven days. [L0360.053]
- Frequency is the main drag on relevance score. The more often one person sees the same ad, the less likely they are to click. [L0510.035]
- Ad frequency, the average number of times a person sees the ad, is the biggest drag on relevance score. Higher frequency lowers CTR and raises CPC. [L1031.027]
- Small audiences of about 1,000 to 5,000 people often hit high frequency quickly with dynamic product ads, which causes CPCs to rise. [L0510.035]
- Aim for a frequency below about 3 as a general guideline. Keep trying to improve it instead of treating three as a fixed target. [L0510.036]
- Watch for a rise in CPC that moves in step with a drop in CTR. That pattern signals ad fatigue. [L0510.036]
- Low frequency, such as 1.03 to 1.2, on large top-of-funnel audiences does not rule out creative fatigue. Negative reactions such as hides and reports can still lower performance, so watch those signals together with frequency. [L0398.006]
- Frequency depends on how long an ad has been running. Compare it against the run length rather than reading it as a fixed number. [L1030.026]
- Frequency is a useful indicator when ads wear out. Use it with hook rate and CTR to decide which creatives to rotate. [L0950.059]
- When spend concentrates on one creative with no results, Meta is signaling that it sees this creative as the best option. Cut that creative from the ad sets and let the budget go to the others to see whether the result changes. [L0361.025]

### Symptom-Cause Diagnosis

| Symptom | Possible causes | IDs |
|---|---|---|
| Great attention + great interest + poor desire / low conversion rate | Ad doesn't clearly sell the product or misleads. Technical problem on the page. Unexpected fees or price shock. Poor product page. Lack of trust. Offer not compelling enough. | L0067.017 |
| Poor attention + great interest + good desire | Test different hooks. Target the right audience more specifically. Research audience language and context. | L0067.017 |
| Great attention + poor interest + good desire | Test better messaging and copy. | L0067.017 |
| Universally weak metrics at all stages | Product is not viable. Kill it and move on. | L0067.017 |

- When CPC, CPM and CTR are all poor, the cause is usually the ad's creative or targeting. Adjust hooks and audience first, rather than changing the offer. [L0874.014]
- When results are poor, first check whether the cause is the messaging rather than the media buying (budget, bidding, targeting). Only then change the buying setup. [L1019.004]
- The ad metrics are read alongside the store. Check the store only after the ad's numbers are healthy. [L0875.029, L0642.010]
- If CTR is strong but there are no sales, the problem is the offer, the product or the website, not the ad. [L0642.010]
- Check CPM and CTR before changing anything else. If CTR is strong but there are no sales, test different offers instead of only changing ads. [L0519.026]

### Diagnostic Sequences for Unprofitable Ads

**Sequence A (Source C version)**

1. If CPM is high but CPC is below 1.50 USD, the audience is expensive but high-intent, so the ads work. Optimize the landing page or the offer. [L1259.008]
2. If both CPM and CPC are high (CPC above 1.50 USD), the audience is too competitive. Test new audiences with more interest testing to find lower CPMs. [L1259.008]
3. If CPM and CPC are high and CTR is low, the creatives are underperforming. Create and test new creatives. [L1259.008]
4. If CPM and CPC are high despite earlier sales, customer feedback may be hurting ad delivery. Improve product quality and customer support. [L1259.008]
5. If CTR is below 1%, test a completely new set of creatives. [L1259.008]

**Sequence B (alternate version, same logic with added steps)**

1. Look at CPM. [L1260.009]
2. If CPM is high but CPC is under 1.50 dollars, the audience is expensive but high quality and the ad is working. Fix the landing page, offer, branding or checkout. [L1260.009]
3. If CPM is high and CPC is over 1.50 dollars, test new audiences with more interest testing to find cheaper CPM audiences. Also improve creatives if CTR is low. Improve product quality and customer support, because negative customer feedback can raise CPM. [L1260.009]
4. If CTR is under 1%, test a new set of creatives. [L1260.009]
5. If CPM is low and CTR is high, the ad is doing its job and the problem is on the website, offer or landing page. [L1260.009]

**Timing rules for these sequences**

- Wait at least one to two days of running ads before optimizing, unless the ad is clearly not profitable. Check Ads Manager first when there are no sales. [L1260.008]
- Ads that have run for two to three days and are still unprofitable should be reviewed after the first or second day. Start with CPM, CPC and CTR in the ads dashboard before touching the website. [L1259.009]

**Account review procedure**

1. Open the Ads Manager and check the CTR of every ad set first, to see whether the creative and audience get attention. [L0650.001]
2. Sort ad sets into performing and non-performing ones, and look inside the weak ones. [L0650.001]
3. Open each ad inside the weak ad set, compare its CTR and spend, and decide per ad. [L0650.001]
4. Give each ad enough spend before judging it. [L0650.001]
5. Pause the losing ads and duplicate the best performing ad set. [L0650.001]
6. If CTR is good but sales are missing, look at the store conversion rate and the offer rather than the ads. [L0650.001]

### Ad Set Diagnosis and Spend Decisions

**Procedure for an ad set with weak results and spend**

1. Check whether one creative takes most of the ad set's spend while performing badly. [L0692.047]
2. If it does, turn off that creative and let the other creatives receive spend up to the break-even cost per purchase. [L0692.047]
3. If spend is then roughly even across creatives and the ad set still performs badly, turn off the whole ad set and test different interests. [L0692.047]

- When new ad sets with different interests are added to a campaign, they can compete for the same people and cause older ad sets to lose sales. If sales drop right after adding ad sets, pause only the new ones for a few days and see whether the old ones recover. [L0950.077]
- A few days of poor Facebook performance can happen even in a healthy account, and earlier good periods do not guarantee stability. Before reacting, cross-check the platform numbers with independent tracking to see whether the drop is real. [L0694.006]

### Breakdown Analysis by Country, Gender and Placement

- Use the Breakdown function to split results by country, gender and page (placement). Find the group with the highest CTR and sales, narrow spending to the groups that sell, and cut the groups that do not. [L0388.032]
- When a product has reached about $300 in ad spend without a sale, run the breakdown analysis before deciding to kill it. Check how the $300 was spread across countries and audiences. [L0388.033]
- Mistake: killing a product because the largest-spend country fails can throw away a viable market. A country that received only about $5 with a strong CTR may just need more budget to show sales. [L0388.034]
- Example: five countries were tested. Australia produced one sale from only about 20 visitors, so more spend was moved there. Sales then came in from that market, which showed the country needed more budget rather than being dropped. [L0388.035]
- If a country gets no sales after the test budget, test the same product in another market. Example: Australia after US and Canada had no sales. The market itself can be the reason for weak results. [L0388.036]
- A country with high CPM can absorb most of the budget and push CPC up. Breaking results down by country quickly shows which country is causing a high CPC, for example the US in Q4. [L0774.029]
- A good CTR combined with a high CPM means the creative catches attention, but reaching the audience in one country costs too much. Remove or separate the expensive country in the ad targeting before replacing the creative. Compare the cost per country in the account breakdown to find it. [L1216.033]

**Procedure after store changes**

1. Pause the ads while you change the store. [L0774.027]
2. Return to Ads Manager and click Breakdown on the ads. [L0774.027]
3. Break the data down by gender, by age and, if you target several countries, by country. [L0774.027]
4. Look for countries with high spend but low CTR or high CPM, and remove or reduce them. [L0774.027]
5. Relaunch new ad sets with the best gender, age and countries. [L0774.027]

### Hypothesis Testing

- Ad analysis is educated guessing from incomplete information. Form a hypothesis by interpreting performance data and creative elements, then test it with a variant to validate whether the theory is correct. [L0008.005]
- No one fully knows what will work. Even experienced mentors give educated guesses based on inference, meaning conclusions reached from available evidence and reasoning. Piece together data and test the hypothesis rather than relying on certainty. [L0067.015]
- Run a control (original ad unchanged) against a variant (one specific change applied). This isolates whether that single change improves performance, so the results measure only that change. [L0008.006]
- When hypothesis testing, keep everything constant except the single element you want to test. Keep the existing ad as a control. Run the test for at least 3 days before evaluating. If sales increase, the change likely caused it. [L0067.014]

**Full diagnostic procedure**

1. Set up your data with proper custom metrics and column types (snapshot and funnel). [L0008.008]
2. Build a complete picture of your ads by reviewing data alongside the actual creative assets. [L0008.008]
3. Apply the AIDA framework to identify which stage (attention, interest, desire or action) is underperforming. [L0008.008]
4. Conduct a creative review comparing your data to your creative elements. [L0008.008]
5. Form an educated hypothesis about what needs to change based on what you found. [L0008.008]
6. Test that hypothesis using a control/variant test structure. [L0008.008]
7. Evaluate the results and decide whether to implement the change, test a different hypothesis, or move on to a new product. [L0008.008]

### Benchmarks by Market and Source

**Where sources disagree:** The sources give different thresholds for the same metric. Each value is kept below with the market or tool it applies to. Use the market's own row, and check every benchmark against your own niche. The T4/T5 values are rough industry averages. [L1259.005, L1354.011]

| Metric | Benchmark | Applies to | IDs |
|---|---|---|---|
| Hook rate | 30% or higher as aim; 40% great; 50%+ excellent | General grading of the first 3 seconds | L0067.006 |
| Hook rate | 45 to 60% good; below about 40 to 45% is a problem in the opening seconds | Video creative check. Use this as the flag for weak openings | L1215.008 |
| CTR | Above 2%, no hard rule | General; depends on product and industry | L0067.007 |
| CTR | At least 1.5 to 2%; about 4% exceptional | Facebook | L0562.003 |
| CTR | Outbound at least 1.5% (some sources cite 2 to 3%) | Facebook outbound clicks | L1212.001, L1212.002 |
| CTR | About 2% or above | T4 and T5 markets | L1259.005, L1260.060 |
| CTR | About 2%; 1.5% minimum | Winning product on Facebook | L0774.051 |
| CTR | Above 1% is good, usually easy to reach | Source E tracker | L1354.012 |
| CTR | Below 1%: test a new set of creatives | Diagnostic trigger | L1259.008, L1260.060 |
| CTR | About 2% | German-language Facebook test | L0694.001 |
| CPC | 0.50 to 1.50 USD is a good range | General; higher acceptable if profitable with good ROAS | L0067.008 |
| CPC | Under 1.50 USD is good; under 1 USD is strong or ideal | T4 and T5 markets | L1259.006, L1260.060 |
| CPC | Healthy range $0 to $3; about $1 very good; about $1.80 still fine; $2 to $3 getting high | Source E tracker | L1354.013 |
| CPC | 0.50 to 0.60 EUR | German-language Facebook test | L0694.001 |
| CPM | Under 30 USD, with CTR about 2% or higher, for a healthy ad set | T4 and T5 markets | L1259.005 |
| CPM | About $20 is healthy; around $60, reevaluate the ads and the creatives | Source E tracker | L1354.010, L1354.011 |
| CPM | Above $25 is somewhat high; $40 or above, close the ad set immediately | Switzerland tests | L0642.014 |
| CPM | About 11 to 12 EUR, with CTR and CPM in range meaning delivery is healthy | German-language Facebook test | L0694.001 |
| CPM | New creatives start at 35 to 40, decline to 20 to 23 by day 4 | New creatives, any market | L0298.054 |
| CPM | Examples of 8 to 9 USD and 20 to 30 USD | Two products from testing | L0502.046 |
| CVR | 2 to 3% | Shopify metrics | L0067.009 |
| CVR | 4% or higher is good | Dropshipping | L0067.009 |
| Frequency | Below about 3 as a guideline | General | L0510.036 |
| Frequency | 1.03 to 1.2 does not rule out fatigue | Large top-of-funnel audiences | L0398.006 |
| Video retention | At least a quarter of views reach 50% | Creative check | L1215.010 |
| Acquisition cost | About 10 to 15 USD per order | Example from testing | L0492.050 |
| Retargeting CTR | 16% (example of a strong signal) | Retargeting audience | L1083.009 |

**Market definitions**

- T4 = USA, Canada, Australia, UK. T5 = New Zealand. [L1260.060]
- T4 and T5 (USA, Canada, Australia, UK, and New Zealand for T5) are the markets for the Source C CPM and CTR benchmarks. [L1259.005]

**Reading the benchmarks together**

- CPM and CTR together: a good CTR with a high CPM is a targeting or country problem, and a low CPM with a high CTR is a website problem. [L1216.033, L1259.007]
- CPC: a high CPM with a CPC under 1.50 USD means expensive but high-intent traffic. [L1259.008, L1260.009]
- If CTR and CPM sit in the German test ranges, ads are being delivered well, and the problem is more likely after the click. [L0694.001]

## Scaling Winning Ad Sets and Campaigns

### Readiness Check and Scaling Triggers

- Know the break-even ROAS before scaling. Break-evens in the lessons range from 1.1 to 1.5 ROAS, and a 3 ROAS at small spend does not by itself make an ad ready to scale. [L0999.028]
- Confirm the campaign setup is correct first. A wrong campaign structure is a reason to stop the ads, correct the structure, and restart. [L1161.027]
- Scale only an ad set that has cleared Rule 4: it spent double the break-even amount and reached break-even ROAS or higher. Scale that exact audience-and-creative combination, because it shows product-market fit for that segment. [L0229.015]
- Start scaling when ROAS reaches break-even + 1. Example: break-even 1.6 gives a scaling threshold of 2.6. [L0642.015]
- Sales-count thresholds differ by source:
  - Scale an interest ad set after 6 to 7 sales while it stays profitable. The 6 to 7 figure applies to testing interests, not to scaling a campaign that already works. [L0693.032]
  - Wait until the ad set has more than six or seven sales. Scaling earlier builds budget on too little data. [L0774.032]
  - About six profitable sales show consistency. [L0646.032]
  - Seven profitable sales is the more reliable scaling trigger. Seven sales in one day is excellent, but seven spread over several days is the typical case. [L1107.027]
  - Five profitable sales is an earlier signal some advertisers use, but seven is more reliable. Sales must be profitable, not just numerous, so check profit per sale before increasing budget. [L1107.037]
  - Alternative rule: start scaling once an ad set has at least 6 to 7 purchases while staying above break-even ROAS, or once it has run for more than 4 days with a profitable ROAS over the last 4 days and at least 6 purchases in that window. [L1335.001]
  - Lighter signal: CTR is good, there are about four to five sales in a few days, and ROAS is strong. [L0650.007]
  - Stricter alternative: wait for five days in a row of consistently profitable sales. [L0693.033]
- Testing CBO entry: run a testing CBO when one asset reaches seven profitable sales at least 0.5 above break-even ROAS. [L0646.025]
- Audience-level gate: scale a winning audience only after at least six sales with ROAS at least 0.5 above break-even, and only once there are at least three winning audiences so the product does not depend on one audience. [L0646.016]

| ROAS signal | What it means | Source ids |
|---|---|---|
| Break-even + 0.5 | Reasonable point to start thinking about scaling | L0646.032, L0646.016 |
| Break-even + 1 (break-even 1.6 → 2.6) | Start scaling | L0642.015 |
| Break-even + 1.5 | Advisable margin for aggressive scaling, where ROAS is expected to be cut in half after doubling | L0646.032 |
| ROAS 2 to 2.5 | Scale slowly, for example doubling 10 → 20 or 30 per day | L0646.019 |
| ROAS about 3 | Raise slowly, about $20 per step | L0999.030, L0999.032, L1335.008 |
| Very strong ROAS | Scaling can be more aggressive | L0646.019 |
| ROAS double break-even | Scale horizontally and check after four days | L0646.026 |
| ROAS far above break-even (1.5 break-even, 3.5 actual), results clearly strong | Duplicate at the same budget or a bigger one such as 50 or 100 | L0693.041 |
| ROAS 1.4 with break-even 1.5, one ad at ROAS 0.5 taking spend | Keep the ad set, switch off the inefficient ad | L0646.036 |

- Keep scaling tied to performance, not a calendar: double the budget as long as ROAS holds. [L0646.013]
- Test the ad level before you judge the ad set. A winning ad set can contain a losing ad that absorbs spend. [L0646.036, L0646.034]

### Frequency and Fatigue Signals

- Sources give different frequency thresholds. Use each for its stated purpose:
  - 7-day frequency of 2 or 3: increase budget so people see the ads closer to once a day. Above 5: no budget increase is needed. At 7 or higher: do not increase spend. [L0360.054]
  - Scaling ad frequency: keep it under 2. At 2 or more, people have seen the ad at least twice and creative fatigue begins, so new creatives go into the same ad set. [L1177.038]
  - Fatigue filter: frequency above 1.5 indicates high audience fatigue. Filter creatives in the Fatigued Ads report in Insights and pause them before ROAS drops. [L0207.016]
- Check frequency after three or four days of a scaling campaign, because frequency rises as the audience sees the same ad again. [L1177.039]
- Facebook now provides fatigue scores for ads in the Ad Manage.ai reporting dashboard. Monitor fatigued ads (declining performance from audience oversaturation) and pause them before ROAS drops significantly. [L0207.008]
- Stop raising the budget when frequency rises or ROAS starts to break. [L0941.058]
- Winning audiences rarely die on their own. A drop usually comes from ad fatigue, rising CPM and falling numbers. Adding new creatives to the same winning audience can revive it. [L0646.050]

### Vertical and Horizontal Scaling

| Method | Definition | Ids |
|---|---|---|
| Vertical | Raise the budget of an existing winning ad set or campaign | L0646.025, L1021.021 |
| Horizontal | Test more audiences, or split spend across several ad sets or campaigns | L0376.013, L1426.002 |

- Horizontal scaling means testing more audiences. While the product stays profitable, launch 2 to 3 new interests per day in the early phase and turn off underperforming ad sets. [L0376.013]
- Horizontal scaling also means splitting spend across several ad sets with different interests and different daily budgets, rather than raising one ad set sharply. Pick distinct interests, because overlapping ones make ad sets compete for the same users. [L1426.002]
- Example: daily budgets of 10, 20 and 25 per ad set for different interests. This worked for a store whose products sold best in peak season, while demand was lower outside it. [L1426.003]
- Scale by adding interest-based ad sets at lower budgets instead of raising a single ad set. [L0644.014]
- Meta's delivery does not spread budget evenly while a campaign is learning. Large budget jumps make the algorithm search harder and can trigger learning-limited status. Scaling in steps keeps delivery more stable. [L1426.004, L0644.015]
- Scaling too fast can get the account capped, or the budget reduced again within a day. One speaker saw this after several raises within 10 days. Raise budgets gradually and monitor delivery after each change. [L1426.005]
- Expect inconsistency when scaling. Find the maximum budget a product can handle before running it steadily. Test both vertical and horizontal scaling, since either can win depending on the week. [L0690.016]
- Winners take off for hard-to-predict reasons, such as hitting a different audience pocket or strong engagement. Test both CBO and ABO and follow whichever produces the money. [L0646.031]
- Allocate about 20 to 30 percent of budget to finding new audiences, so new winners keep appearing. Relying on one audience is risky. [L0646.022]
- Sequence: scale vertically first, then horizontally. Add new ad sets only once the existing campaign budget is stable. [L1021.021]
- Changing scaling campaigns too often stops Meta from collecting enough data. Make changes deliberately and let the campaign stabilise between adjustments. [L1021.020]
- A stalled example: a seller duplicated an ad set horizontally and then vertically, and sales stalled at about $4.5K to $5K per day. The seller suspected the algorithm became confused. [L1127.026]
- Scaling method and horizontal/vertical choice:
  - After five days of a profitable CBO, scale horizontally by duplicating the campaign at 50, 100 or higher, or vertically by raising the budget 20 to 30 percent. [L0646.025]
  - If ROAS is double break-even, scale horizontally and check after four days. If still profitable, scale again. Vertical scaling should be cautious, with changes at four-day intervals relative to break-even. [L0646.026]
  - If a raise (e.g., 65 to 70 dollars) is unprofitable after four days, go back down. If the lower budget (50 per day) is still unprofitable after another four days, turn the campaign off. Test both methods, since the best one varies by account. [L0646.027]
  - Scaling a winning ad set has three possible routes, and the best one should be tested per account: (1) raise the ad set budget step by step, about every three days while profitable; (2) duplicate the winner as a CBO with a budget like $50, running three days; (3) duplicate the ad set several times inside the same campaign (for example four ad sets at $5 each) for horizontal scaling. [L0642.017]
  - Scaling a winning ad set is also possible by raising its budget 20 to 25 percent, or doubling it to about 20 units. Duplicating the winner with a higher budget such as 20, 30 or 50 is the other route. The choice depends on available budget and how aggressive the scaling should be. [L0646.018]
- Example of scale path: from 1k to 5k to 10k to 50k per day by increasing budget while the algorithm finds the right customers, with good creative systems and account structure. [L0296.003]
- Simpler account structures work better in some cases: prospecting ad sets plus retargeting, scaled by budget. [L0650.009]

### Budget Step Rules and Cadence

**Step ladders**

| Ladder | Condition | Ids |
|---|---|---|
| $5 → $10 → $20 → $50 → $100 → $250 | Raise about every three days while profitable | L0642.017 |
| $5 → $10 → $20 → $50 → $100 → $200 → $500 | Standard winner ladder; if results drop, switch to 20% steps | L0773.031, L0774.031 |
| $5 → $10 → $20 → $50, or 20% per step | Two ways to scale; test which fits the account | L0209.004 |
| $10 → $20 → $50 | Ad set in ABO testing campaign | L0693.034 |
| $10 → $20 → $50 → $100, or 20% per step | Winning ad set, when CTR, offer and store conversion are good | L0650.007, L0650.060 |
| $5 → $10 → $20 → $50 → $100 | Aggressive ABO scaling while profitable | L1316.015 |
| $20 → $50 → $100 → $200 | Aggressive pattern used by some advertisers; faster than 20%, riskier | L0693.042 |
| $100 → $200 → $400 | Profitable ABO ad moved into a scaling campaign | L0999.030 |
| $10 → $50 → $100 or $200, or 2x to 3x | Duplicating a winning ad set at a higher budget | L1335.034 |
| $20 step per increase | Ad with ROAS about 3 on small spend | L0999.069, L0999.032 |

**Percentage rules**

- 15 to 20 percent steps are considered safe because they do not push the campaign out of learning or damage optimisation. [L1016.058]
- 20 percent is the safe step. Larger jumps can disrupt optimisation, so avoid sudden budget changes that reset learning. [L0693.040]
- The 20 percent rule exists to scale slowly. If scaling aggressively, use duplication or doubling, which avoids learning-phase resets. [L0298.010]
- 20 to 25 percent every 48 hours avoids resetting learning. Avoid sudden large increases, which cause fluctuation. [L1335.033]
- 20 percent every 48 hours, only when the campaign stays consistently above break-even ROAS. [L1335.006]
- Conservative ABO: raise the ad set budget 20 percent every second day instead of duplicating. The right method depends on the account. [L1316.014]
- Scaling by 20 percent every one to two days, stopping when frequency rises or ROAS starts to break. [L0941.058]
- Winner in ABO audience testing: increase by about 20 percent every two to three days, or every three to four days. [L0398.038]
- Vertical CBO step: 20 to 30 percent. [L0646.025]
- Vertical scaling in four-day intervals relative to break-even. [L0646.026]
- Budget formula: starting budget × 1.2 for each increase. From 100, two increases give 144 (100 × 1.2 × 1.2). [L1161.053]
- Check the arithmetic before planning steps. A daily budget step from 5 to 7 is a 40 percent increase, not 20 percent. [L1017.022]
- Doubling: if ROAS is around 2 to 2.5, scale slowly, for example from 10 to 20 or 30 per day. [L0646.019]
- Doubling as long as ROAS holds, so the step follows performance rather than a fixed calendar. [L0646.013]

**Cadence: the sources disagree**

| Cadence | When it applies | Ids |
|---|---|---|
| Every 24 hours | Not recommended; the 48-hour rule replaces it for learning reasons | L0298.009 |
| Every 48 hours | Default safe interval for profitable CBO and learning protection | L0298.009, L1335.006, L1335.033 |
| Every second day | Conservative ABO | L1316.014 |
| Every 1 to 2 days | Slow scaling with stop rules (frequency or ROAS) | L0941.058 |
| Every 2 to 3 days, or 3 to 4 days | ABO audience winner | L0398.038 |
| About every 3 days | Step ladder while profitable; ad-level ramp | L0642.017, L0999.030 |
| After every fourth day | ABO performing ad sets; reduce if results slow | L0646.014 |
| Four-day checks | Vertical and horizontal changes relative to break-even | L0646.026, L0646.027 |
| Day five, then every four days | CBO start | L0693.039 |

- Same-day increase, older guidance versus recent experience:
  - Older guidance held that raising a winning ad set's budget by more than 25 percent every two to three days resets the learning phase. [L0646.020]
  - Recent experience: a same-day increase during strong momentum (for example 4 to 5 ROAS with more than 3 sales) boosted performance without resetting the algorithm. [L0646.020]
  - Same-day doubling during strong momentum tends to boost performance, but a raise of 25 percent or more could in the past reset the algorithm. [L0646.024]
  - Test the current behaviour in each account before relying on either rule. [L0646.020]
- Any change to an ad set resets the learning phase. The reset is acceptable during events but should be avoided on normal days. [L0298.011]
- Waiting 48 hours between 20 percent adjustments (not 24) prevents resetting learning. [L0298.009]
- Large jumps reset learning: the budget can be reset by Facebook so spend falls back the next day. Scale in smaller steps and check the budget after each change. [L0644.017]
- Going down: if an increase is unprofitable after four days, return to the previous budget. [L0646.027]
- Changes that remove the budget entirely cause instability. Budgets cut from about $1,000 per day to zero and restarted at $100 to $200 gave unstable results. Change budgets gradually. [L1127.027]
- Raising the budget of a proven ad set reaches more people within the same audience, which brings more quality traffic and sales. Raise only when CTR, the offer and store conversion rate are already good. [L0650.060]
- Budget ladder for a newly profitable product: start a CBO at 50 USD per day if ROAS is acceptable; consider 100 USD only if ROAS for that interest is very high. [L0693.035]
- Scaling a profitable ad on ABO with a small budget: start at $20 per day, run a few days, and add another $20 if it stays profitable. Repeat about every three days. [L0999.069]
- Duplicating a winning ad set at a higher budget (for example $10 to $50 to $100 or $200, or 2x to 3x) is covered in the duplication section. [L1335.034]

### Duplication Methods

- Older guidance: duplicating ad sets was effective two years ago but no longer works well, so treat it as a deprecated scaling method. [L0209.001]
- Conflicting guidance: duplicating winning ad sets is still used in several structures below. Duplicates compete with each other in the same auction, but a copy can reach a different group or gain engagement. [L0646.030]
- Duplication with CBO delivers more consistent scaling than budget increases alone. Duplicate from 200 to 500 dollars of budget for campaigns with 4+ ROAS. [L0298.005]
- Duplicating a winning ad set into a CBO and running it three days is one route. [L0642.017]
- Duplicating a winning ad set four times inside the same campaign (for example four ad sets at $5) is the horizontal route. [L0642.017]
- Duplicating an ad set to a higher budget keeps the same audience and creatives, so the winning combination stays intact. [L1335.034]
- Duplicate a winning ad set into a new campaign with the budget type set to Advantage campaign budget (formerly CBO), a minimum daily budget of $50, and four ad sets with the same audience and creatives so the algorithm can find different audience pockets. Leave ad-set budgets empty. [L1335.005]
- Duplicating an ad set on a higher budget and a fresh set of creatives: create a new ad set with a fresh batch of creatives rather than adding to a working ad set. [L1225.062]
- Ways to use duplicates:
  - Duplicate the winning ad set as a CBO with a daily budget of 20, then duplicate that CBO three times for a total of 60 dollars per day. Kill weaker CBOs once they have spent about 50 dollars, and keep raising the winner from 20 to 50, 100, 200 and so on. [L0646.012]
  - Once an audience is a winner, create a CBO for that audience with four ads using the same audience and creative, starting at 100. [L0646.021]
  - Duplicate the winning asset four times into a new CBO. [L0646.025]
  - Duplicate a winning ad set into a CBO with 20 budget, after building the ad set to 5 sales and then 10 budget. [L0646.011]
- Triple duplication case: three CBO triple-duplication campaigns, each with the same ad set at $100 ($300 total), were stopped after about $50 to $70 each with zero sales, leaving about $200 spent. Earlier, a single interest in a testing campaign had about 20 sales and very high ROAS, but in a CBO scaling campaign it returned under 1 ROAS. The response was to check the setup, verify results with UTMs, and retest after a couple of weeks at a different budget such as $200 or $50. [L0349.033]
- Other ad sets targeting the same interest usually lose delivery and results when one ad set is scaled. Keep testing ad sets separate from scaling ones. [L0524.038]
- Never turn off a winning interest that runs in ABO just because you launch a CBO with it. Both can run side by side, and audiences of 30 million or more rarely overlap enough to matter. [L0645.014]

**Procedures**

Procedure: Scale a winning audience by duplicating (ABO audience-testing campaign)

1. Increase the budget by about 20% every two to three days, or every three to four days. [L0398.038]
2. When the performance drops, do not edit the ad set; duplicate the same audience. [L0398.038]
3. Replace the creative in the duplicate, or paste in the new winning creative. [L0398.038]
4. If the duplicate also fails, turn it off. [L0398.038]

Procedure: Scale a winning interest in ABO testing (horizontal copy)

1. Keep the winning interest ad set in the ABO testing campaign. [L0693.034]
2. Raise its budget step by step, for example from 10 to 20 and then to 50. [L0693.034]
3. Alternatively, duplicate the ad set inside the same testing campaign with a higher budget, such as 50. [L0693.034]

Procedure: Duplicating a winning ad set at a higher budget

1. Duplicate the ad set. [L1335.034]
2. Set a higher budget, for example from $10 to $50 to $100 or $200, or 2x to 3x. [L1335.034]
3. Change only the budget and start time. [L1335.034]
4. Keep the audience, creatives and all other settings identical, since the winning combination must stay intact. [L1335.034]

Procedure: Horizontal scaling of a winning ad

1. Take the winning ad set and place it in a separate scaling campaign by itself. [L0999.031]
2. Duplicate it, for example 10 times as ABO ad sets with $20 each. [L0999.031]
3. Turn off the ad sets with no sales after the test period, usually about one day with no sales. [L0999.031]
4. Keep the ad sets that perform well and let them scale. [L0999.031]

Procedure: Testing and scaling a new interest with a small budget

1. Start a new audience at 5 units of budget in a single-interest ad set. [L0646.011]
2. Once the ad set reaches 5 sales, raise its budget to 10. [L0646.011]
3. Leave the ad set running for two to three days at least. [L0646.011]
4. If orders stay consistent with good ROAS, start scaling by duplicating the ad set four times into a CBO with a 20 budget. [L0646.011]

Procedure: Second testing method (ABO)

1. Start with multiple similar interests in one ad set at 5 dollars. [L0646.014]
2. Duplicate that ad set a few times within the same testing campaign to increase spend. [L0646.014]
3. Compare results. [L0646.014]
4. Increase the budget of performing ad sets by 20 percent after every fourth day, and reduce it if results slow. [L0646.014]

Procedure: Duplicate a winning asset into a CBO

1. Create a CBO campaign for the audience with four ads using the same audience and creative. [L0646.021]
2. Launch it at 100 as a start budget. [L0646.021]

Procedure: Testing and scaling procedure for CBO

1. Run a testing CBO when one asset reaches seven profitable sales and at least 0.5 above break-even ROAS. [L0646.025]
2. Duplicate that asset four times into a new CBO. [L0646.025]
3. Keep the campaign with the most sales and kill the rest. [L0646.025]
4. Start each at 50 or 100 dollars depending on how high ROAS is. [L0646.025]
5. After five days of a profitable CBO, scale horizontally by duplicating the campaign at 50 or 100 or higher, or vertically by raising the budget 20 to 30 percent. [L0646.025]

Procedure: Testing and scaling procedure for CBO (three-CBO close-out)

1. Duplicate the ad set into a CBO campaign. [L0774.031]
2. Run three CBOs. [L0774.031]
3. Close the two weakest and scale the budget of the winning CBO. [L0774.031]

### CBO Scaling Structures

- Use a CBO with exactly five ad sets inside as a secondary scaling method when other approaches fail. [L0209.002]
- Scaling failures are caused by the scaling method used, not the business manager or ad account. Switch scaling approaches rather than replacing accounts. [L0209.005]
- Scale with CBO only when both the audience and the creative are proven. CBO removes ad set and creative variables and gives Facebook control over where to spend. [L1177.037]
- Scaling setup: a CBO with a daily budget of about 100 dollars, one ad set with the same winning interest, and one winning image creative. 80 dollars is acceptable as a lower start. [L1177.036]
- Austrian/Australian case: when scaling a winning product in Australia, a CBO tested one narrow audience: women 45 and older with an interest in astrology, in Sydney and Melbourne. These were the best-performing locations, so the budget was concentrated there. [L0388.044]
- Stacked interests in one CBO work only when each winning interest gives consistent strong results. With only a few sales, stacking all interests together may not work. [L0646.023]
- CBO results can be inconsistent across days. An account may perform well for a while and then drop. Check results over several days before deciding a structure works. [L0650.061]
